# United States v. SSG Saintloth: case feedback analysis

**Basis and limits.** Built from three sources: the defense slide deck ("Details for DFE with messages"), the Preliminary Hearing Officer (PHO) report of 10 Aug 2026 and, from section 22 on, the government's 26-page evidence packet, which holds the complete undercover log. Not reviewed: the interview video, the phone extraction, the ad, any forensic report. I am not a lawyer and this is not legal advice. Statements of law are my understanding, and defense counsel should confirm each one.

**Updated 24 Nov 2026.** Later sections take further material in the order it was supplied. Section 8: the deck's timeline slide and one interview statement, as relayed and not as heard on the video. Section 9: the two-day timeline and the "Ok. Where at?" point. Section 10: the single account of May 1 the defense then put forward. Section 11: three further points, and whether the image search counts as a disclosure that she was an adult. Section 12: a college email posted on the profile, and how the undercover number appeared in the texting app. Section 13: his responses to points in this analysis, including that he had meant to block the number. Section 14: the provider's help pages, and the record of the habit. Section 15: his answers to the questions in 14.4, which reopen whether her conversation had been deleted. Section 16: his answers to the cross-examination questions in section 6. Section 17: his response on how this text is to be used, with each point sorted by the record that supports it. Section 18: whether hypnosis could be used to recover his memory. Section 19: four objections to this analysis, which lead to three corrections and a restatement of his account in his own terms. Section 20: a fifth point, on his nervousness at the hotel and what it shows about intent, with the reported cases found. Section 21: Spec 3 element by element, with what the government has to prove at a general court-martial and where its proof is strongest and weakest. Section 22: the evidence packet, read directly, with the DPS report, the vehicle inventory and the Army CID report set against the complete log. Section 23: the search for comparable cases, with the outcome of each and whether it was appealed. Section 1 gives the current position. Sections 2 to 7 have been revised where later material changes them.

## 1. Bottom line

*Current as of section 23.*

**The charges**

- **Spec 3** (attempted patronizing, as amended) is strong for the government, and age is irrelevant to it. It is strongest on the acts and the money. It is least proved on the kind of act he intended, on how firm the intent was, on what he had with him and on the terminal element. Those are points for counsel, and his testimony would probably close them (section 21). **Spec 4** should fall away: the PHO found no probable cause and the government said it would dismiss.
- **Specs 1 and 2** turn on one question: on May 1, did he believe the person he was texting was under 16? There was no real child, so these are attempts, and an attempt is judged on the facts as the accused believed them. "Should have realized" is not the test. In the excerpts provided, age is never mentioned on May 1.
- **Part of Spec 1 has an answer that does not depend on her age.** As relayed, the specification alleges that he communicated a wish to ejaculate on her, and that rests on his "Yes." The log shows "Yes" arriving two seconds after the line it is said to answer. That reading stood for months, until he was given the log and noticed the times (section 19.1).

**His account as it now stands**

He says that more than one thing was true at once, and that forcing one answer onto each moment is what went wrong in the interview (section 19.2). In his terms:

- **What he did not have.** A plan. Any knowledge of whose number he was texting. Any thought, at any time on May 1, that he was dealing with a minor.
- **What he did have.** A routine: numbers from adult ads, the same opening lines, the location and the photos checked, the app installed and removed (section 17.3). A suspicion that this contact was a scam, from an earlier experience at the same hotel. An urge he describes as compulsive, which took him there anyway.
- **What he does not know.** How her number came to get the message. He thinks it more likely that he answered her ad again, or sent the same line to several recent conversations, than that he opened one wrong conversation. Whether he had deleted her conversation. What was on the screen when he typed.
- **April 30.** Told she would be 16 in June, he refused within 37 seconds. He searched the ad's photo and found an adult's profile (sections 12.1 and 13.3). He meant to block her number (section 13.5).
- **The interview.** What he told the agents was a tidy, general version of an afternoon that had no single explanation. He says he told them to look at his phone (sections 16.5 and 19.2).

**What supports it**

- The refusal. It is the government's own exhibit, and it shows what he did the one time he believed she was a minor.
- Nothing was said about age on May 1, and the two days' conversations read differently (section 16.1).
- The timing of "Yes" (section 19.1).
- The parts of the account fit each other, and fit most of the texts without any special reading (section 19.2).
- The government's papers have said more than the log does at several points. Most were caught only by reading the log against them (section 19.1).
- If the timeline's entries are right, her conversation was the next one down in a list sorted by recent use, directly below the five he had already messaged that day (section 19.2).
- Everything he was shown was an adult: the ad's photos, and the profile they traced to, which states an age of 23 and carries a 2021 college email (sections 12.1 and 13.3).
- Records he describes but has not yet produced: the app installed and removed on earlier dates, earlier image searches, and the provider's page read before April 30 (section 17.2).
- An inference for counsel: a man who checks photos, looks for signs of a sting and stops at once when told "16" is unlikely to go back to that number knowing whose it was (section 19.3).
- The room was her proposal. The log shows him wary from the moment she named the hotel, almost an hour before he said he was nervous, and nothing I have seen shows him going to the room (section 20.1).
- The government's own papers now confirm that he refused because of her age, that the pictures he was sent were of adults, that the room, the second person, the price and the explicit words were the agent's, and that he did not go up (section 22.9).
- April 30 read in full. The age came after the price, the payment and "Im ready now," and he gave it all up in 37 seconds. The agent did not repeat the age the next day (section 22.2).
- Military appeals courts have said in terms that in a case with a decoy the government must prove he actually believed she was under 16 (section 23.5).

**What stands against it**

- Whichever way the message was sent, something of hers was in front of him: her conversation, with the April 30 lines in it, or the ad whose photo he had searched the day before (sections 15.2 and 19.2).
- The interview statement, as relayed, has her as one person on both days. A mixed state of mind does not explain that. Either the agents had told him by then, or he knew (sections 8.2 and 19.2).
- On his own account he had picked out her number in order to block it, perhaps that same afternoon (sections 13.5 and 19.2).
- He has said that the second photo resembled the profile he had found the day before, or possibly the ad (section 10.4).
- Compulsion is the government's explanation too: the urge that overrode the refusal (sections 3 and 19.2).
- What he did at the hotel, and the removal of the apps minutes after he described his car (sections 9.3 and 14.3). After he said he was nervous, each message moved toward a meeting on his own terms, and the offer about the car was his (section 20.2).
- In the reported cases found, hesitation in a sting was read against the accused wherever a court discussed it. None of them matches his facts (section 20.4).
- Three of his answers replace the natural meaning of a message: "100" as a time, "Cash right?" as meant for someone else, and the extra as nothing but a test. The records go against them, and the account does not need them (sections 16.1 and 19.2).
- The first step of the account has changed again, from a wrong recipient to an unknown one (sections 13.6 and 19.2).
- Routine and compulsion both concede that he was pursuing paid sex, which is Spec 3 (sections 17.3 and 19.3).
- "Cash right?" at the hotel follows "Prefer cash or cashapp?" and "cash" in the same conversation the day before. "Awesome" follows the photo he had asked for as proof (section 22.2).
- In every comparable case found, an accused who refused and then went on was convicted, and entrapment failed. In each of them he knew whom he was going back to (section 23.5).

**What it asks a fact-finder to accept on his word**

1. That he did not know whose number it was, with her conversation or her ad in front of him.
2. That, if he knew the face in the second photo from the profile, he did not recall the conversation that had led him to it.
3. That what he told the agents that evening was not what he had thought.

Each is possible. Needing all three is the difficulty. The first is the only point on which Specs 1 and 2 need a single answer. Everything else in his account can stay as mixed as he says it was (section 19.2). Records can take some of the weight off the first, and the video may do the same for the third.

**What will decide it**

1. The interview video: the exact words, the form of the questions, what he had been told by then, and whether he told the agents to look at his phone (sections 8.2, 16.5 and 19.2).
2. How the message came to be sent: whether the same line went to other numbers within minutes, whether her ad was open, what her conversation held and what the screen showed (sections 15.2 and 19.2). The provider's copy may no longer exist (section 14.2).
3. The exact words of Spec 1 and of Spec 3, and what the examiner says the timestamps record (sections 19.1 and 21.3).
4. Where he was, and how he moved, while the messages were sent. The arrest is now fixed at 5:02 pm, in the lot where he had parked, the same minute his timeline gives for the removal of the apps. Which came first, and what he had with him, are still not recorded (sections 19.2, 22.5 and 22.6).
5. The second photo and the advertisement (sections 11.2 and 11.3).
6. His earlier conversations and trips: whether May 1 looks like the rest (sections 17.5 and 19.5).

**Texas.** The offense there can rest on what he was told, so the search may not bear on guilt (section 11.4). DPS announced the charge as solicitation of prostitution of a person under 18, which is that offense (section 22.8).

**The evidence packet.** I have now read the government's 26-page packet. The log is complete and matches what this analysis has used. The two police reports say more than the log does, and the "admission" is one second-hand sentence that does not say which day it refers to. The arrest was at 5:02 pm. The inventory does not show what he had with him (section 22).

**Comparable cases.** The case he gave to start with, *United States v. Caroll*, ended two months ago at Fort Hood in conviction on the same two charges and a sentence of 16 years. No court-martial was found with a reverse image search, and no military opinion with a claim like his about memory. Where an accused refused and came back, he was convicted. The nearest cases to his account are civilian, with one court-martial known only from a defense firm's report, and they were decided on records (section 23).

**Urgent.** The provider says a number lapses after 30 days without use, and that message history may not survive long inactivity. Nearly seven months have passed. Counsel should be asked now whether the provider's records were preserved or obtained, by the defense or by the government (section 14.2). The profile and the Google account's activity can also be lost or changed (sections 11.5 and 13.7). So can location data, which Google now keeps on the phone itself and deletes after three months by default (section 19.2).

**Hypnosis.** He has asked whether a hypnotherapist could help him recall the events. A court could not refuse to hear him for that reason alone. But hypnosis does not make recall more accurate, it would open his testimony about what he believed to a new attack, and it cannot be undone. He should have no session about these events before counsel has advised (section 18). He has since said that its purpose would be to show honesty. It would show sincerity, which nobody would doubt, and not accuracy (section 19.4).

## 2. Prosecution

### Strengths

1. **Age was disclosed and understood.** "i will be 16 in june is that ok" drew "No. Find a different way of life please." He cannot claim he missed it.
2. **He initiated the return.** Same UC number, same thread, about 23 hours later. Nobody lured him back. On the defense's present account he opened that thread by mistake, which still puts the April 30 exchange in front of him (section 12.2). If the thread had been deleted, as he now says is possible, the number was entered some other way (section 15.2).
3. **May 1 shows intent to complete a paid encounter.** He offered an Uber, answered "Ok. Where at?" 18 seconds after "2 for 1 tonight 100," sent "Yes" and "Just you….then both," asked for pictures, drove about 20 minutes, asked "Cash right?", asked to be walked upstairs, wrote "I have tinted windows if you can carplay. Ill give extra," and identified his vehicle. Travel to the meeting place is a textbook substantial step. Section 20.3 gives the authorities on travel, and a military decision that went the other way. Section 21 sets out the elements of Spec 3 and where this proof is thinnest.
4. **Concealment evidence, some of it supplied by the defense deck itself.** Tinted windows, nervousness, the peace-sign verification request, burner-number app searches, removal of the texting app in the parking lot, and a pre-arrival search for sting indicators. He has since said the habit predates these events and has given a reason for the app searches (sections 13.1, 13.5 and 14). Section 20.4 gives a federal decision in which items of this kind were used to show that the accused knew the age.
5. **A recorded interview after a signed rights waiver.** DPS reports he "admitted to knowingly speaking to a minor," and he admitted prior patronizing. The relayed statement that he "thought the next day she just needed money" supplies the link the texts lack: that on May 1 he knew who he was texting (section 8).
6. **The complete log.** One conversation, the same two numbers, times to the second. On April 30 he asked "Prefer cash or cashapp?" and was told "cash," and at the hotel on May 1 he wrote "Cash right?" After the photo he had asked for as proof he wrote "Awesome" (section 22.2).
7. **The reported cases.** Where an accused refused on learning the age and then went on, the conviction was upheld and entrapment rejected (section 23.5).

### Weaknesses

1. **No ad in the evidence packet.** The government told the PHO he answered an ad for an underage child. The PHO rejected that for lack of evidence and found he did not know the age until told. I have now read the packet, and it has no ad (section 22.1).
2. **Nothing about age on May 1.** The UC never re-confirmed age in the conversation that matters. The PHO found only that he "had reason to believe" she was 15, which is a probable-cause finding and not proof beyond a reasonable doubt of actual belief.
3. **The April 30 refusal is the government's own exhibit.** It was immediate and moral. The PHO's word "hesitated" undersells it.
4. **On May 1 the explicit words are the UC's.** His are "Yes," "Just you….then both" and "carplay." "An amazing bj" predates the age disclosure. Spec 1 (indecent language) depends on reading short replies in context.
5. **The "Yes" timing.** It posted 2 seconds after "on both of us lol" and 24 seconds after "ok u gonna cum," which followed his own "Its 20 min away from me." A "you gonna come?" reading is available. As relayed, Spec 1 rests on this message, and the reading stood for months until he noticed the times. Section 19.1 treats it as an answer to that part of Spec 1 that does not depend on her age.
6. **The PHO's intent reasoning is thin.** It infers specific intent "because" the conduct was a substantial step, and it treats the $50 agreement as if it came after the age disclosure. It came before, and he withdrew. At trial, intent must be proved on its own.
7. **The DPS summary of the "admission" is second-hand.** It comes from an agent who was not in the room, and the PHO, who watched the video, cited it only for prior patronizing "but not minors" and for marital status. If nothing on the video says he knew or believed on May 1 that she was 15, the summary overstates. This is a smaller weakness than it first appeared, because the relayed statement lets the government argue the same point from his own words (section 8). The packet gives the sentence in full: the writer "was not present" and "was informed," and it does not say which day is meant (section 22.7).
8. **Polygraph evidence is inadmissible (M.R.E. 707),** which complicates how the interview is presented.
9. **The police reports overstate the log.** The Army report says that he "believed" she was 15, that he agreed to pay $100 for "two minor females," and that he made statements about ejaculating on both. The log supports none of these in those terms (section 22.3).
10. **The interview paperwork.** The packet has a Texas waiver and a polygraph consent, with no time on either, and no Army rights form, although the DPS report says that Army CID did the interviewing (section 22.7).

## 3. Defense

### Strengths

1. **The 37-second refusal.** It is direct evidence of what he does when he knows the age.
2. **The legal standard.** The government must prove what he believed, and its proof for May 1 is inference. Section 11.3 sets out the standard and one open question about it.
3. **Everything he saw was adult.** By his own search, the photos trace to a 23-year-old's account, and the PHO found his history was with adults, "not minors." The profile also carried a posted college email from 2021 (section 12.1).
4. **Digital corroboration, if it holds.** The image search 11 minutes after the April 30 chat ended; other profiles messaged on May 1 before the UC (the two-day timeline lists five between 13:48 and 15:30, all still awaiting forensic confirmation); hotel lookups in Round Rock and Georgetown at 3:18 PM, half an hour before the UC text; the same stock opener to other numbers ("Are you still in killeen?", "Hi are you still available?").
5. **An innocent reading of the caution.** Paying an adult for sex is also a crime and a career-ender for an NCO. Nervousness, deletion and tinted windows do not distinguish between the two. Section 20 sets out how reported cases have treated hesitation of this kind, and what would show the reason for his.
6. **The PHO's recommendation.** A neutral judge advocate recommended a bad-conduct discharge with no confinement. It is non-binding, but it is leverage in negotiation.
7. **April 30 in full.** The age came last, after a price, a way of paying and "Im ready now. Which hotel?" He gave up a finished arrangement at once. The agent, with that refusal on the screen, did not repeat the age the next day (section 22.2).
8. **The standard, confirmed.** Three military appeals courts have said that in a case with a decoy the government must prove the accused actually believed the persona was a child (section 23.5).

### Weaknesses

1. **Too many theories, and they fight each other.** A panel that hears all of them is likely to believe none. Section 8.3 explains this in full. Section 10 tests the single account the defense has since put forward. His answers to the cross-examination questions bring several of them back (section 16.2). Section 19.2 records his objection that several of them can be true together, and sorts them.
   - "I didn't realize it was the same person" conflicts with "my image search told me she was an adult." The second means he recognized the ad he had researched right after being told 15.
   - "The photos showed an adult" conflicts with "I knew the photos were fake." Fake photos say nothing about who is texting.
   - "I did not intend to meet anyone" conflicts with the drive, "Cash right?", "Can you walk me upstairs?" and "Ill give extra."
   - Three separate misreadings ("on" as "or," "will" as "we'll," "Ok" answering only "we have a room") look contrived when stacked.
2. **The linchpin is unproven.** The deck says he "can't definitively remember" deleting the April 30 thread. If the extraction shows the thread was still there at 15:49:15 on May 1, the refusal exchange sat directly above the box he typed in. Uninstalling the app cannot explain the mistake either, since the UC log shows the May 1 text arriving in the same thread from the same sender. The defense now says the thread was in the app's list and he opened it by mistake (section 12.2). That makes what the screen showed the central forensic question. The provider says a deleted conversation cannot be recovered (section 14.2). He has since said the thread was possibly deleted, which would change how the number was reached (section 15.2).
3. **His diligence cuts against him.** A man who reverse-searches photos, demands verification and checks for stings is hard to believe when he says he did not notice a number from 23 hours earlier. This has less force if, as now relayed, the conversations were listed by number only (section 12.2).
4. **The image search began within minutes of the age disclosure.** Expect: "You were checking whether she was really 15, looking for a reason to say yes." He now says it was started during the conversation (section 13.4).
5. **"Compulsion" serves the government's story too:** the urge overrode Thursday's "no." It is mitigation for sentencing, not a defense to intent. Section 17.3 separates addiction from habit, which records can prove.
6. **Self-inflicted exhibits.** Searches for second-number apps at 2:01–2:04 PM on May 1, including a visit to "Vikk – AI Lawyer & Legal Help" at 2:02 PM; the claimed pre-arrival sting search; removal of the texting apps at 17:02, minutes after he described his car. Each needs a truthful explanation before the government sees it. He has since given a reason for the app searches (section 13.5). Section 14 covers the provider's help pages and the record of the habit.
7. **Timestamp errors in a deck that depends on timestamps.** Slide 2 lists the re-contact as 15:47 (log: 15:49:15) and the image searches as 16:42, 16:47 and 16:50 (the screenshot shows 4:54 and 4:58 PM). The two-day timeline corrects both, so slide 2 should be withdrawn. Slide 9 still lists "will be down" as 16:56:57 (log: 16:58:57).
8. **"Physically impossible" overstates a good point.** "My reply was already on its way" is enough.
9. **Late arrival.** None of this was presented at the Article 32 hearing. That is his right. But the one interview statement relayed so far says he thought she needed money. Unless the video also has him saying "I thought it was someone else" or "I looked her up and she was 23," expect the argument that the deck's accounts were built afterwards from the phone records.
10. **Entrapment and voluntary abandonment are weak.** He re-initiated, stayed in the lot and identified his car. Section 20.3 sets out the rule on abandonment. The reported cases bear this out (section 23.5).

## 4. How a panel or jury is likely to view the evidence

**Who decides**

- **Court-martial.** Military judge alone, or a panel of members senior to him (enlisted members if he requests). Panels tend to follow instructions on burden and intent carefully and to hold NCOs to a high standard. Conviction takes three-fourths, not unanimity, so one holdout does not help, but there is no hung panel either.
- **Bell County.** Twelve jurors, unanimous, in a military community. Texas Penal Code § 43.021(b)(2) makes solicitation a second-degree felony if the person was younger than 18, was *represented* to him as younger than 18, or was believed by him to be younger than 18. If he knew on May 1 that he was texting the person who had said "16 in june," the "represented" branch may be met whatever the image search led him to believe. The DPS draft cites § 43.031(b)(2), which looks like the wrong section for a buyer. Texas counsel should confirm what is actually charged.

| Evidence | Prosecution reading | Defense reading | Likely reaction |
|---|---|---|---|
| April 30 refusal | He knew her age | He refuses minors | Helps him. Then they ask: "So why go back?" |
| "Are you still around?" 23 hours later | Deliberate return | He did not know whose number it was: a stock line sent to an ad or to recent conversations | A familiar kind of carelessness, so they will consider it. Then it depends on how the message was sent and what was on the screen (sections 15.2 and 19.2) |
| No age question on May 1 | He already knew | No reason to ask | Follows whichever story they already believe |
| "Ok. Where at?", "Cash right?", the drive | Agreed and acted | "Ok" answered "we have a room," not the price; "100" read as a time; "Cash right?" meant for someone else | Reads as intent to buy sex. The room reading is arguable for "Ok" alone (section 9.5). The two newer readings are likely to cost more than they gain (section 16.1) |
| "Yes" / "Just you….then both" | Agreed to the act; chose the 15-year-old | "Yes" answered "are you coming?" and arrived two seconds after her second line; "Just you" answered the offer of two | The timing should defeat the charged reading of "Yes," if someone presses it (section 19.1). "Just you" reads as sexual interest in that persona |
| "tinted windows… carplay… Ill give extra" | Hidden sex with a child, for more money | Afraid of the room; a test to expose a scam | Most damaging text. A bluff or test will not be believed unless he can say what he meant to do once she agreed; "afraid to go in" might (section 16.1) |
| "Im a little nervous", "Can you walk me upstairs?", no approach to the room | Nervous because he knew she was 15; watching for the police | The room was her idea; he was wary from the moment the hotel was named, and he would not go in | They will accept that he was afraid of the room. They will also see that he then asked her to come to the car. It says little about her age unless the reason is shown (section 20) |
| Image search to an adult profile that states an age of 23, with a posted 2021 college email | Still focused on her after "15"; the owner is in Rhode Island, so the photos were borrowed | All he saw was an adult, by her own stated age | They will accept that the owner is an adult. The question is whether he took her to be the person texting (sections 11.3, 12.1 and 13.3) |
| Other recipients, stock phrases, hotel lookups | Prolific buyer | Lost track | Makes a mix-up plausible; costs sympathy |
| Deleted threads, searches for second-number apps, app removal | Consciousness of guilt | Removing the app was his routine before April 30; the app searches were to avoid texting her number by accident; adult prostitution is illegal too | If the records show the routine, the removals stop looking like a reaction to her, though the timing at 17:02 still draws questions. The reason given for the app searches cuts both ways (sections 13.5, 14 and 15.1) |
| Interview: "thought the next day she just needed money… just giving money and leaving" | He knew on May 1 who she was, and knew he should not be there | He refused and denied wanting sex; he was describing how he usually thinks, not what he remembered | Outweighs anything said later, unless the video shows he was speaking generally or had just been told who she was. The texts undercut the "give money and leave" half (section 13.2) |
| The account as restated in section 19: no plan, recipients unknown, a scam suspected, compulsion, never a thought of a minor | He knew the number, and the urge beat the refusal; the explanation came after the arrest | One state of mind with several parts, resting on a routine the records can show | Easier to follow than the earlier versions, and it needs fewer special readings. It stands or falls on whether they accept that he did not know whose number it was (section 19.2) |
| Addiction | Urge beat scruple | Explains carelessness; what he did on May 1 was his routine with adults | As "addiction," little value on findings and some at sentencing. The routine itself can be proved from records and is worth more (section 17.3) |
| April 30 in full: the age came after the price, the payment and "Im ready now. Which hotel?" | He wanted exactly this until he was told, and he named the act | He gave up a finished arrangement in 37 seconds | Helps him more than the summaries did. It also shows that he read a bare number as a price (section 22.2) |
| "Cash right?" at the hotel, after "Prefer cash or cashapp?" and "cash" the day before | He remembered what she had told him, so he knew who she was | A question he asks every number | Depends on his other conversations. If they show the same question, it is a habit. If not, it is the one message on May 1 that looks back to April 30 (section 22.2) |
| "Awesome," after the photo he asked for "so I know real" | He was satisfied that she was real, and came | He still expected a scam | They will read it as satisfied unless something else explains it (section 22.2) |
| The agents' reports and the "admission" | He believed she was 15 and admitted it | The reports say more than the log, and the admission is second-hand | The panel will not see the reports. It will see the log and the video, and hear the agents cross-examined on the differences (sections 22.3 and 22.7) |

## 5. If he testifies

**The decision.** It is his, made with counsel. For: a state-of-mind defense is hard to mount without him. Against: cross-examination on every text and on the interview, the prior patronizing, and the fact that sworn testimony in one forum can be used in the other (the Army and Texas are separate sovereigns). A mock cross-examination with counsel should come first.

**Ground rule.** He should testify only to what he actually remembers and believed. I will not suggest wording for anything untrue, and nothing loses faster than an account the extraction or the video contradicts. Where the deck reconstructs rather than remembers, the honest answer is "I don't remember," and the forensic examiner carries the point. Section 18 explains why hypnosis is not a safe way to recover more.

**Points to make, in this order, if they are true**

1. **Own the bad fact first.** "I was trying to pay an adult for sex. It was wrong and I knew it was illegal." Counsel should weigh conceding the Spec 3 conduct; it buys credibility for the fight that matters. Section 21.5 sets out what the concession gives up.
2. **April 30, plainly.** He answered an ad showing an adult. When told "16 in June," he said no and meant it.
3. **The image search.** What he did and the one real reason he did it. That includes how much of the profile he looked at (section 12.1).
4. **May 1.** How many ads he contacted, where this number came from, what was on the screen when he typed, and what he truly believed about who he was texting. The first draft of this point had him say he did not know it was the person from the day before. The relayed interview statement points the other way. If he did know, that line cannot be used, and his testimony has to start from what he told the interviewer (section 8). The defense position is now that the message was meant for a woman he had texted earlier that day (section 10). If that is what happened, this is where he says it, and the phone records have to carry it. He has since said that he does not know how the message came to be sent, and thinks an ad or a round of messages more likely (section 19.2).
5. **The parking lot.** He was nervous about police, robbery and exposure because buying sex is illegal, not because of age.
6. **The interview.** Only after watching it. He will have to explain "she just needed money" and "just giving money and leaving" in his own words. If he never mentioned the image search that night, he will also have to say why. Section 13.2 assesses the explanation since offered.

**Claims that will cost credibility.** Keep one only if it is a real memory, and expect it to be attacked.

- "I did not intend to meet anyone."
- "Physically impossible."
- Reading "on" as "or."
- Carplay as a "bluff test."
- "Indifferent" to whether the encounter was genuine.
- Addiction as the explanation for May 1. His routine is a different matter, and records can prove it (section 17.3).
- Reading "100" as a time (section 16.1).
- "Cash right?" as meant for someone else, unless the other conversation shows it (section 16.1). The April 30 exchange about cash makes this harder (section 22.2).
- "It seemed harmless at the time," beside "nervous" and "paralyzed" (section 16.4).

## 6. Likely cross-examination

Each entry gives the question, an honest shape for the answer, the prosecutor's rebuttal, and the reply. Every answer is conditional on being true. Answers 2 to 5 assume he did not know on May 1 that he was texting the person from April 30. The interview statement in section 8 suggests he did. If he did, those answers are not available to him. Section 10.6 lists the questions the later account will draw. Section 11.6 adds those raised by what he remembered of the profile. Section 12.4 adds those raised by the college email and the unsaved number. Section 13.4 assesses the answers he has since given to the questions in 12.4. Section 14.4 adds those raised by the provider's help pages and the record of the habit. Section 15 assesses his answers to those in 14.4. Section 16 assesses the answers he has since given to questions 4 to 18. Section 19 records four objections to this analysis and what they change.

1. **"On April 30 you were told she was 15, and you understood?"**
   - Answer: "Yes. That's why I said no."
   - Rebuttal: "Then 'Not in rude way.' You kept the door open."
   - Reply: "No. I had just told a kid to change her life and didn't want to sound cruel."
2. **"23 hours later, same number: 'Are you still around?' 'Still,' because you had talked before."**
   - Answer: "I texted that number. I did not know it was the same one. That is how I open with ads; I sent the same kind of line to other numbers."
   - Rebuttal: "Same phone, same app. Your 'No' was on the screen above it."
   - Reply: depends on forensics. Either "The thread was gone; the screen was blank," or "I don't remember seeing it. I delete those threads. Had I seen it I would not have sent that."
   - Rebuttal: "You don't remember deleting it."
   - Reply: "Not that specific thread, no." Concede it and stop.
   - Update: the account now given is that he opened this conversation by mistake from a list of unsaved numbers (section 12.2). On that account "the thread was gone" cannot be the reply. What remains is what he remembers seeing, and what the examiner can show the screen displayed. He has since said he does not recall seeing his earlier words (section 13.4). The provider says a deleted conversation cannot be recovered (section 14.2). He has since said the conversation was possibly deleted. If it was, the screen was blank, but the number did not come from the list. He cannot say which from memory, so the records have to (section 15.2). His present account returns to the first answer above: he did not know whose number it was (section 19.2).
3. **"Where did you get the number on May 1? The same ad you reverse-searched the day before?"**
   - He must know this cold: which site, which ad, what he recognized.
   - Rebuttal: "The only reason you searched that photo was that she said she was 15."
   - Reply: whatever the true reason was. This is the hardest link in the mistaken-identity account.
4. **"You check photos and demand verification, but you didn't notice the number?"**
   - Answer: "I check pictures, not numbers, and I had texted [number] ads that week." The count must come from the extraction.
   - If the conversations were listed by number only, with no names, he can say that too (section 12.2).
5. **"You never asked her age on May 1."**
   - Answer: "I had no reason to think it was an issue. The one time it was, I ended it in under a minute."
   - Rebuttal: "Or you already knew."
6. **"'2 for 1 tonight 100.' 'Ok. Where at?' You agreed to pay $100 for two girls."**
   - Answer, if true: "I had asked if I could send an Uber. She said no, they had a room. I said ok and asked where it was. I was not answering the price."
   - Rebuttal: "You read the price. You never said no to it. At the hotel you asked 'Cash right?' and offered extra."
   - Reply: concede what is true, that he did not object and later talked about cash, and stop. He should not claim he did not understand "2 for 1."
   - Follow-up: "Why did you want to know where the room was?" The answer has to be the truth, and it has to fit everything else he says about meeting her.
7. **"'u gonna cum on both of us lol.' 'Yes.'"**
   - Answer: "I had just said it was 20 minutes away. I read the first line as 'are you coming' and answered yes. The second line arrived two seconds before mine posted."
   - Rebuttal: "Then you wrote 'Just you….then both.' You never corrected it."
   - Reply: "By then I understood she meant both of them. I was saying who I wanted to see." That concedes intent toward an adult, not knowledge of age.
   - Update: as relayed, Spec 1 rests on this exchange. Section 19.1 sets out what the timing removes and what it leaves.
8. **"You drove 20 minutes and asked 'Cash right?'"**
   - Answer: "Yes. To meet an adult."
   - Update: this answer supplies the intent that Spec 3 requires (section 21.5).
   - Update: on April 30 he asked "Prefer cash or cashapp?" and was told "cash" (section 22.2). Expect: "She told you cash the day before. That is why you wrote 'Cash right?', isn't it?" The answer is whatever is true. His other conversations will show whether he asks every number.
   - If he claims scam testing: "Then why drive there? Why offer extra?"
9. **"'I'm a little nervous.' Nervous because she was 15."**
   - Answer: "Nervous because buying sex is illegal, could end my career, and could have been a robbery or police."
   - Rebuttal: "You searched for stings and burner apps that day."
   - Reply: "For the same reason." He must know the exact search terms before trial. He has since given a different reason for the app search (section 13.5), so this reply now fits only the sting search. He has since changed the answer above it to a scam, which changes what this reply says (section 16.1).
   - Update: section 20.4 gives the one reported case found in which nervousness was used to show knowledge of age. The accused there had said so himself, and had said he was not nervous with adults.
10. **"You offered a 15-year-old extra money for sex in a car with tinted windows."**
    - Answer, if true: "I was afraid to go into that room, so I asked her to come to the car. I believed she was an adult."
    - The bluff version draws: "A bluff with more money? And when she agreed, you described your car."
11. **"You removed the app in the parking lot. You were destroying evidence."**
    - Answer: "I delete that app and those threads out of shame, and had many times before." This needs forensic proof from before April 30 (section 13.1). He has since said the Google activity shows it for the app. On his own answers many conversations from April 30 were still in the list on May 1, so the answer should not describe deleting threads as a routine unless the records show it (sections 14.3 and 15.2).
    - Rebuttal: "But you didn't drive away."
    - Reply: the truth about why.
    - Update: the arrest was at 5:02 pm, the minute his timeline gives for the removal. His last message was at 17:01:20, so after it he had no time to drive away. The question still stands for the thirteen minutes before (section 22.6).
12. **"That night you told the agent: 'I refused and thought the next day she just needed money.' 'She' is the girl who told you she was 15?"**
    - Answer only after watching the video, and only the truth. "The question had no time frame" no longer works, because the sentence supplies its own: "the next day."
    - If he says he did not know it was her: "Then who is 'she'? Who 'just needed money'?"
    - If he says he knew but believed she was 23: "Where on this recording do you tell the agent she was 23?"
    - Update: the DPS report's sentence about an admission was written by an agent who was not present (section 22.7). The video is what matters.
13. **"You say you thought about just giving her money and leaving. Then why 'Just you….then both'? Why 'Cash right?' Why 'Ill give extra' for the car?"**
    - No answer shape can be offered here. Whatever he says has to fit all three texts.
    - Follow-up: "And why give money and leave at all, unless you knew she was someone you should not touch?"
14. **"Your memory failed a few hours after it happened, but it is reliable today?"**
    - Answer, if true: "I was exhausted and frightened that night and I described it badly. For what I did, I am pointing to the phone records, not to my memory."
    - Rebuttal: "The records show what you looked at. Only you can say what you believed, and that night you said she needed money."
15. **"That same night, in over an hour, you never said you thought it was someone else, and you never said you had looked her up."**
    - The video controls. If he said either, counsel plays it. If he said neither, this may be the hardest question in the case.
16. **"The woman in the photos is 23, but you asked for a peace sign 'so I know real.' You thought the photos were fake, so they told you nothing."**
    - This needs one consistent truth about what he believed the photos showed.
    - Update: the second photo shows a peace sign held across the face, and he answered "Awesome" (section 22.2). Expect: "You asked for proof she was real. You got it. You wrote 'Awesome.'"
17. **"How many times have you paid for sex?"** (if the judge allows it)
    - Answer: short and truthful. "With adults. Never a minor, and when one came up I refused."
18. **"You say addiction. So the urge overrode your judgment, as it overrode Thursday's 'no'?"**
    - Answer: "It made me careless about who I was texting. It has never made me want a child."
19. **"'Im ready now. Which hotel?' That was you on April 30, before she told you her age. And the next day you asked the same number, 'Are you still around?'"**
    - Answer, if true: "Yes to the first. When she told me, I said no. The next day I did not know whose number it was."
    - This question comes from the complete log (section 22.2). The reply to the second half is the one in question 2.

## 7. Before any decision

1. **Watch the full interview with counsel.** Transcribe every statement about age, identity, scam belief and intent. Section 8.2 lists what to look for around the "needed money" statement. Get the Army CID interview record as well; the DPS report says CID questioned him separately.
2. **Get the forensic report before fixing a theory.** Thread deletion time; app install and uninstall times; how the UC number was entered on May 1; the page viewed at 15:45–15:49; the number of ads contacted April 28 to May 1; exact search queries; the date of the Play Store screenshot. Section 12.2 adds tests for the accident account. Section 13.7 lists records that would support the newer details. Section 15.5 adds to it.
3. **Demand in discovery:** the ad (text, stated age, photos, site), the operation plan and any age-confirmation protocol, the full UC extraction with metadata, and the arrest inventory (cash on hand). Section 20.3 explains why the inventory matters. Section 22.11 lists what the packet shows is still missing, including the jail's record of his property and money.
4. **Settle on the one true account and correct the timestamps.**
5. **Motions for counsel to assess:** suppression of the interview (the DPS report describes a joint DPS–Army operation, which raises an Article 31(b) question), M.R.E. 707, M.R.E. 404(b) on prior patronizing, the rule of completeness for the interview, and speedy trial under R.C.M. 707 (charges were preferred 24 June 2026). Section 21.3 adds points on Spec 3: the wording of the specification, the definition of a sexual act, and proof of the terminal element. Section 22.7 sets out what the packet shows about the interview paperwork.
6. **Coordinate military and Texas counsel.** Testimony or a guilty plea in one forum is usable in the other. The PHO's suggested deal does not say which specifications; a plea to Spec 1 or 2 would almost certainly bring sex-offender registration.
7. **Ask counsel about a psychosexual evaluation,** which is often used in sting cases for negotiation and sentencing. Section 17.3 notes how the condition is classed.
8. **Keep work product inside the defense team.** Material shared outside the attorney-client relationship may not be privileged, and anything handed to a testifying expert can reach the prosecutor. Route the forensic examiner through counsel. Section 11.5, point 6, cites a 2026 federal ruling on exchanges with AI tools.
9. **Secure what can disappear.** The texting provider's records for his account, and a capture of the profile as it stands. Nearly seven months have passed since the events (sections 11.1 and 11.5). The Google account's activity should be preserved as well (section 13.7). The provider's stated policy on unused accounts makes this more urgent (section 14.2). Location data for both days should be traced and preserved as well (section 19.2).
10. **No hypnosis about these events before counsel has advised.** It does not make recall more accurate, it can change what he remembers, and it gives the government a new line of attack on his testimony (section 18).
11. **Put each point from the exhibits on one page and give it to counsel.** The exhibit, the lines, the times, and what the government's papers say instead. As relayed, the reading of "Yes" behind Spec 1 stood for months because nobody had done this (section 19.1). Section 22.3 does it for the Army report.
12. **Go through the comparable cases with counsel** (section 23): the record in *Caroll*, the other soldiers arrested in the same operation, and the decisions on how a panel is instructed about belief.

## 8. Addendum, 21 Nov 2026: timeline, interview statement, and the contradiction point

**What this covers.** No new attachment arrived with the request, so this section reviews the timeline slide already in the deck (slide 2) and the detail supplied with the request. If a separate timeline document was meant, it did not come through. The interview quote is used as relayed. I have not seen the video, and the exact words matter.

**Short version**

- The timeline's value is that the phone recorded, before the arrest, that he was shown an adult's profile. That proves what he looked at. It cannot prove what he believed.
- The interview statement hurts more than the timeline helps. It treats the person he texted on May 1 as the person he refused on April 30, which is the fact the government most needed and the deck most denies.
- "Poor recall" is a weak explanation for a statement made a few hours after the events, and "the phone records disprove it" claims more than records can show.
- The contradiction problem is now worse, because the interview adds an account that conflicts with most of the deck.

### 8.1 What the timeline adds

This subsection reviews slide 2. Section 9 reviews the fuller two-day timeline supplied afterwards, which corrects the times in point 6 below and marks the "other recipients" as assumptions still awaiting forensic confirmation.

Merged sequence, Central time:

| When | What | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Apr 30, 16:33 | Conversation begins | Deck timeline |
| 16:40:09 | UC: "i will be 16 in june is that ok" | UC log |
| 16:40:46 | "No. Find a different way of life please" | UC log |
| 16:42:25 | "Not in rude way." | UC log |
| 16:43:04 | UC: "k i get it." Conversation ends | UC log |
| 16:42 to 16:50, or 16:54 to 16:58 | Reverse image search; views an X profile whose bio gives the woman's age as 23 and her location as Rhode Island. As later relayed, he also viewed a posted photo of a 2021 college email (section 12.1) | Deck timeline gives the first range; the Google activity screenshot shows the second |
| May 1, 13:49 to 15:45 | Texts to at least four other recipients | Deck slide 4 |
| 15:18 | Hotel lookups in Round Rock and Georgetown | Google Maps activity |
| 15:49:15 | "Are you still around?" to the UC number | UC log (deck timeline says 15:47) |
| After 16:59 | Arrest, then the interview the same day | PHO report; rights waiver dated May 1 |

**What it adds for the defense**

1. **The refusal came first and owed nothing to the search.** He said no 37 seconds after "16 in june," on her word alone.
2. **A record he could not have created later.** The phone logged the profile view before the arrest. It is the only defense evidence about what he knew that predates the arrest, so it is the hardest to call an invention.
3. **A gap in the government's account.** The PHO and DPS summaries run straight from "told 15" to "came back." The records put a search in between, and they show a May 1 that included at least four other recipients and hotel lookups in other towns. That supports arguing that the government's account is too simple.

**What it does not do**

1. **It records an act, not a belief.** It shows which page he opened. What he concluded from it is still only his word.
2. **It bears only on May 1, and only through a link the deck also denies.** The timeline's own note concedes the profile view came after the April 30 chat ended. So it can matter only to what he believed the next day, and only if he connected the person he texted on May 1 with the photo he had searched. Item (e) says he did ("sees image that reverse image links to twitter") and, in the same sentence, that he did not realize it was the same person. Section 8.3 takes this up.
3. **What the search found points to borrowed photos.** The profile is the personal account of a woman in Rhode Island. The natural conclusion is that the ad used her pictures. That is what he acted on the next day ("Send a pic with peace sign so I know real") and what slide 8 says ("identifying fake photo"). Borrowed photos say nothing about the age of whoever was texting from Belton.
4. **The 23 hours in between are blank.** Nothing shows how the number reached his phone on May 1: tapped from an ad page, typed in, or picked from recent messages. Until the forensic report shows which page was open at 15:45 to 15:49 and how the message was started, item (e) is an assertion.
5. **The deck gives two versions of how the May 1 text was sent.** Slide 3 says "Are you still around" was meant for someone else, prompted by the 15:18 conversation, and went to the UC by mistake. Timeline item (e) says he saw the ad's picture again and contacted that ad, without connecting it to the day before. In the first, her picture plays no part. In the second, he recognized it. Only one can be what happened. The defense has since chosen the first (section 10).
6. **The times do not match the exhibits.** The timeline puts the searches at 16:42 and 16:47 and the profile view at 16:50. The Google activity screenshot on slide 1 shows the image views and the profile result at 4:54 PM and the handle search at 4:58 PM. The request now says the search came after the conversation, but 16:42 falls inside it, in the same minute as "Not in rude way." A time-zone display setting or entries missing from the screenshot could explain the gap. The forensic report should settle it, because a search begun at 16:42 lets the prosecutor say he was looking her up while still talking to her. He has since said the search was started during the conversation (section 13.4).
7. **It sets up a hard question about the interview.** If by 17:00 on April 30 he believed she was 23, why does the statement he gave the next evening say she needed money, and not that she was an adult?

### 8.2 The interview statement

As relayed: "I refused and thought the next day she just needed money so the next day I thought about just giving money and leaving."

**What it gives the government**

1. **Identity.** "She" is one person across both days. The government no longer has to prove that the April 30 thread was still on his screen.
2. **Belief, by inference.** A man who thinks he is meeting an adult escort has little reason to hand over money and leave, and nobody feels sorry for a scammer. The thought fits best with someone he believed he should not touch.
3. **Continuity with his own text.** "Find a different way of life please" on April 30 and "she just needed money" in the interview show the same concern for the same person. That makes the statement look like memory, not confusion.
4. **Timing.** He said it the same day, before he had a lawyer and before he had seen the evidence. Fact-finders trust that kind of statement over a later one.
5. **A half they can disprove.** "Just giving money and leaving" runs into "Just you….then both," "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra." The government will ask the panel to accept the half that hurts him and reject the half that helps, and the texts allow it.

**What it gives the defense**

1. **It repeats the refusal and denies wanting sex with her.** If the government plays part of the interview, the defense can ask to bring in the rest.
2. **It may not contain the word "minor."** The DPS line that he "admitted to knowingly speaking to a minor" may be an agent's reading of this sentence. If nothing on the video says he knew or believed on May 1 that she was 15, counsel can show that the report overstates. The government will still play the clip and argue the inference, and it is the inference most listeners would draw.
3. **It shows conflict, not predation.** In his own words on the day: refusal, sympathy, second thoughts. With the PHO's finding that his history involved adults, "not minors," that is strong material for negotiation and sentencing even if it does not win Specs 1 and 2.

**Why "poor recall" is a weak explanation**

- The interview was on May 1. "The next day I thought about just giving money and leaving" describes that same afternoon.
- The error claimed is not a wrong time or a forgotten detail. It is describing as familiar a person he now says he did not recognize. People rarely get that wrong within hours.
- The deck concedes poor memory now (slide 3: "I can't definitively remember"; slide 10: "I don't remember saying"). The prosecutor will ask why his memory failed that evening but is reliable today.
- A more specific explanation would carry more weight, but only if the recording bears it out. The recording will show how long he had been questioned, whether the "needed money" idea came first from him or from the interviewer, and whether he was describing what he thought before the arrest or what he understood after being told whose number it was. Section 13.2 assesses the one since offered.

**Why "the phone records disprove it" claims too much**

- The records and the statement are not opposites. Both can be true: he saw the profile on April 30, and on May 1 he still thought of her as the girl who needed money.
- Where they pull apart, the defense says the statement is wrong and the government says the new theory is wrong. The government has his own same-day words on its side.
- If he believed that evening that she was 23, the natural thing to tell the agent was "I looked her up and she is 23." Whether he said that anywhere in the interview is now one of the most important facts in the case.
- "Disprove" invites the same damage as "physically impossible." What the records fairly support is narrower: the investigators' summary skipped an intervening event, and he had been shown that the photos belonged to an adult.

**What to look for on the video**

1. The exact words, and the question asked just before them.
2. Who first said "needed money," or anything like "help her": he or the interviewer.
3. Whether he mentions looking her up, a profile, "23," or believing she was an adult.
4. Whether he says anywhere that he did not realize it was the same number.
5. When he says the "give money and leave" thought came: before texting, on the drive, or in the parking lot. The timing matters, because the texts the PHO relied on for Spec 1 were sent at 15:59 to 16:00 and at 16:58, with the drive in between.
6. Whether the statement came before, during or after the polygraph, and how long he had been held by then.
7. The separate Army CID interview: any recording, notes or signed statement.

**Points for counsel** (my understanding; each needs confirming)

- **Completeness.** If the government offers part of the interview, M.R.E. 304(h)(2) lets the defense introduce the remaining portions.
- **Impeachment survives suppression.** Under M.R.E. 304(e), a statement excluded for an Article 31 or rights-warning violation can still be used to contradict the accused's own testimony. Winning a suppression motion would not make it safe to testify to something the statement contradicts.
- **Polygraph circumstances.** If the statement came out of the polygraph session, *United States v. Kohlbek* (C.A.A.F. 2019) held that M.R.E. 707 does not bar evidence about the circumstances of the examination when it is offered to explain why a statement was made.
- **Belief or suspicion.** The image search may show at most that he was unsure of her age. Whether the government must prove he actually believed she was under 16, or whether going ahead while unsure is enough, should be answered before a theory is chosen. Section 11.3 takes this further.
- **Texas.** § 43.021(b)(2) reaches a person "represented to the actor as being younger than 18." If he knew he was texting the person who had said "16 in june," what he came to believe afterwards may not matter there. Section 11.4 takes this further.

### 8.3 "The slide deck's explanations contradict each other," explained

**Why this outweighs any single exhibit.** Specs 1 and 2 are decided by what was in his mind on May 1. The texts show what he did. The only evidence of an innocent belief is his own account, so the case rests on whether he is believed. Someone who gives several accounts that cannot all be true is usually believed on none of them, including the parts that are true. Prosecutors also argue that shifting explanations show consciousness of guilt.

**The deck gives six answers to one question.** The question is what he believed and intended on May 1.

| Name | The answer | Where |
|---|---|---|
| Wrong person | I did not realize it was the number from the day before | Slides 2(e), 3, 4 |
| Adult | My search showed she was 23 | Slide 1 |
| Scam | I believed nobody real was there, and I was testing | Slides 7, 8, 9 |
| No intent | I did not intend to meet anyone | Slide 9 |
| Misread | The texts do not mean what they appear to say | Slides 5, 6, 7, 9 |
| Compulsion | I kept going without thinking | Slides 4, 7, 8 |

The interview adds a seventh: I knew who she was, felt sorry for her, and thought about giving money and leaving. The request adds an eighth: what I said in the interview was poor recall.

**Where they collide**

1. **Wrong person against Adult.** The search was run on the photo of the person who said "16 in june," within about a quarter of an hour of her saying it. Its result could shape what he believed on May 1 only if he knew he was texting that person. If he did not know, the result is irrelevant. If he did know, Wrong person is false. Slide 2(e) tries to keep both: he recognized the picture as the one that led to the adult profile, but not as the one whose owner had said she was 15. That asks the panel to accept that, 23 hours later, he remembered the answer and forgot the question.
2. **Wrong person against itself.** Slide 3 says the text was meant for someone else. Slide 2(e) says he meant to contact that ad. See 8.1, point 5 under "What it does not do." The defense has since chosen the slide 3 version (section 10), so slide 2(e) should be withdrawn.
3. **Adult against Scam.** To believe the texter was the 23-year-old in the photos, he had to believe the photos were hers. Scam says he had identified the photos as fake, and his own text asks for a peace-sign picture "so I know real." If the photos were not hers, they told him nothing about her age.
4. **Scam and No intent against what he did.** A man who thinks he is texting a scammer and does not intend to meet anyone does not drive 20 minutes, ask "Cash right?", ask to be walked upstairs and offer extra for the car. Slide 8 also calls him "indifferent" to whether the encounter was genuine, which is different from believing it was fake.
5. **No intent against Adult.** Adult explains why meeting her would not be the charged crime. No intent denies wanting to meet at all. A man who believed he was meeting an adult has no need for the second, and offering both suggests neither is firm.
6. **"Give money and leave" against the rest.** Slide 9 says that when signs pointed to a real person he "thought maybe I should only give money and leave." The interview says much the same. That thought makes little sense toward an adult escort or a scammer. It makes sense toward someone he believed he should not touch, which is what the other slides deny.
7. **The misreadings.** There are three, and each falls on exactly the message that hurts: "on" read as "or," "will" read as "we'll," and "Ok" answering only the earlier line. One misreading is believable. Three in a row, all in his favor, look like explanations written afterwards. They also leave the problem in place: on slide 7's own account, "Just you….then both" was his answer to the two-girl offer. A fourth has since been offered: "100" read as a time (section 16.1).
8. **Slide 1's argument turns around.** It says that if he had known of the previous day's age, he would have asked a question to clear up the discrepancy. The interview indicates he did know who she was, and he asked nothing. On the deck's own reasoning, that silence now counts against him.
9. **The interview against Wrong person and Scam.** "She" is the same person on both days, and nobody feels sorry for a scammer.

**How it plays in cross-examination.** Each collision becomes a question with no good answer, such as "Which is it: you didn't know it was her, or you knew and thought she was 23?" After two or three of those, the panel stops listening to the parts that are true and that help him: the refusal, and a history that involved adults only.

**The way out.** One account, and it has to be the true one. A workable test: it can be told in three or four sentences; it matches every text, every phone record and the video; and every exhibit the defense offers supports it. Anything that does not fit is dropped, however appealing on its own. Because the interview statement came first, the account has to begin with it, not go around it. Section 19.2 records his objection that more than one thing can be true at once. One account can have several parts. It has to be the same account each time.

### 8.4 What this changes

1. **The risk on Specs 1 and 2 is higher than the first draft assessed.** The first draft treated "did he know who he was texting" as open. The relayed statement goes a long way toward closing it against him.
2. **The decision to testify is harder.** If he knew it was the same person, he cannot say otherwise on the stand, and the statement can be used against any testimony that contradicts it.
3. **The phone evidence is better used for a narrower purpose.** It supports "the investigators' summary is incomplete" and "he had reason to doubt she was 15." It does not support "he did not know who she was."
4. **Negotiation deserves a serious conversation with counsel.** The PHO recommended a bad-conduct discharge with no confinement. The refusal, the tone of the interview and the "not minors" finding carry weight in that setting. Which specifications a plea would cover, sex-offender registration and the Texas case all belong in that conversation. The decision is his. A guilty plea to Spec 1 or 2 in the court-martial would require him to admit under oath that he believed she was under 16 (section 11.5, point 3).
5. **The first step is unchanged.** Watch the interview with counsel before committing to any account.

## 9. Addendum 2, 21 Nov 2026: the two-day timeline and "Ok. Where at?"

**What this covers.** The two timeline images supplied after section 8 (April 30, items 1 to 9; May 1, items 10 to 27), and the defense position that "Ok. Where at?" answered "we have a room" and did not accept an offer.

**Short version**

- The timeline corrects the earlier timing errors and adds useful context. Its grey entries, which include every message to another person, are assumptions until the forensic examiner confirms them.
- It gives the government as much as it gives the defense. The UC was contacted last, after five others. The texting app was installed seven minutes before the first UC message. The apps were removed about three minutes after he was asked what car he was in.
- Reading "Ok. Where at?" as a question about the room is arguable, and its meaning is for the fact-finder. It is a low-value fight, because "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra" show agreement to pay without it.
- The hotel search supports only the part nobody disputes, that "where" was a real question about location. It is also a step in planning the trip.

### 9.1 The May 1 afternoon, timeline and UC log together

| Time | Event | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 13:11 | Texting app reinstalled | Assumed |
| 13:48 to 15:30 | Messages to five other profiles (Jane Does 1 to 5) | Assumed |
| 14:04 | Search for a new texting app | Verified |
| 15:45 to 15:50 | Gas and food stop, E Stan Schlueter Loop | Shown as verified |
| 15:49:15 | "Are you still around?" to the UC | UC log |
| 15:53:50 | "Ok. Where at?" | UC log |
| 15:57 | Searches the Belton hotel | Verified |
| 15:58:25 | "Its 20 min away from me" | UC log |
| 16:13 to 16:23 | Walmart, Harker Heights | Shown as verified |
| 16:48 | On scene; the door photo with the room arrives at 16:48:45 | Shown as verified; UC log |
| 16:49 | Tries to park at the La Quinta | Shown as verified |
| 16:51:08 | "Cash right?" | UC log |
| 16:58:19 | "I have tinted windows if you can carplay. Ill give extra" | UC log |
| 16:58:57 | UC asks what car he is in; he answers "Black suv" | UC log |
| 17:02 | Texting apps uninstalled | Shown as verified |
| 17:02 | Arrest, by the jail card (section 22.6) | Evidence packet |

On April 30 the timeline shows the app installed at 16:26, the first UC message at 16:33, the conversation ending at 16:43, the image search at 16:54, class work from 18:30, and the phone off until 22:10 (assumed).

### 9.2 What it adds for the defense

1. **The times now match the exhibits.** The search is at 16:54, after the chat ended at 16:43, and the re-contact is at 15:49.
2. **April 30 ended with ordinary obligations.** After the search he went to a class presentation, and there was no further contact with the UC that day.
3. **May 1 looks like an ordinary day with a round of contacts.** Rent, a tire appointment, Walmart, and six profiles messaged, of which the UC was one. Two of the others (Jane Does 1 and 2) were also people he had messaged the day before. If confirmed, going back to the previous day's numbers was his routine, which supports "Are you still around?" as a stock line.
4. **It separates what is verified from what is assumed.** That is the right habit, and everything the defense produces should follow it.

### 9.3 What it adds for the government

1. **The UC was contacted last.** Five other profiles between 13:48 and 15:30, then the UC at 15:49. The government's reading is that he tried everyone else first and went back to the one he had refused when nothing else worked. That fits the interview statement and the deck's own "compulsion" account. The order that shows "one of many" also shows "last resort." Section 19.2 notes another reading: her conversation may simply have been the next one down the list.
2. **Going back to yesterday's numbers means he kept track of them.** To message Jane Does 1 and 2 again he needed their numbers or threads from April 30. The prosecutor will ask why the third April 30 contact was the only one he did not recognize.
3. **The app was installed at 16:26 and the UC was messaged at 16:33.** That suggests the app was set up for this contact, which reads as planning and concealment. It fits a habit of installing the app for each use just as well (sections 13.1 and 14.3).
4. **The number was the same on both days.** The UC log is one thread, so the May 1 messages came from the April 30 account. Many texting apps keep conversations on their servers and restore them at login. If this one does, reinstalling at 13:11 would have brought the April 30 exchange back, unless he had deleted that thread. The examiner should identify the app and how it behaves. Section 12.2 takes this further. The provider's help pages have since answered both questions (section 14.2).
5. **A search for a new texting app at 14:04.** It is verified, and the timeline does not explain it. Expect to be asked why he wanted another number that afternoon. He has since given a reason (section 13.5).
6. **Steady movement toward Belton.** Gas stop, hotel search, "20 min away," Walmart, on scene. That is a deliberate trip, and it undercuts "I did not intend to meet anyone." Expect to be asked what he bought at Walmart and whether he took out cash. Receipts and bank records will show it either way. He says there was no plan, and the records before 15:58 fit that (section 19.3).
7. **The La Quinta at 16:49.** Trying to park at another hotel a minute after arriving reads as watching for police or a set-up. That fits caution about any illegal meeting and says nothing about age. It is not what someone does who has decided not to meet. A federal appeals court has counted watching the meeting place among the signs that an accused knew the persona's age (section 20.4).
8. **The uninstall at 17:02.** It came about three minutes after the UC asked what car he was in and he answered. The government will call that destroying evidence while waiting for her to come down. "Disengaging" is more persuasive if he was driving away. The arrest report and video will show the time of arrest and whether the vehicle was moving. Section 13.1 covers the habit he says this was part of. Section 14.3 covers the record of it. The jail card gives the arrest as 5:02 pm, the same minute (section 22.6).

### 9.4 Gaps and inconsistencies to fix

1. **Slide 4 against the timeline.** Slide 4 says four other recipients were "verified" at 13:49, 13:56, 15:18 and 15:45. The timeline shows five, at 13:48, 14:06, 14:58, 15:18 and 15:30, all marked as assumptions. The 13:56 message in slide 4's screenshot, sent from the regular messaging app, is not on the timeline.
2. **The basis for the grey entries.** They are inferred from hotel searches. A hotel search shows he was looking at hotels. It does not show a message, a recipient, or that the recipient was an adult. Until the examiner or the app provider's records confirm each one, the defense should not describe them as texts.
3. **Grey entries before the install.** Items 1 and 2 are assumed to be texting-app messages at 12:22 and 15:17 on April 30, but the app is shown as installed at 16:26. Either there was an earlier install or those messages went another way.
4. **The sting search is missing.** Slide 8 refers to a "pre-arrival internet search specifically looking for signs of a sting operation." It is not on the timeline. Its time and exact wording matter.
5. **Unexplained or untimed entries.** Item 24, "text from unknown" at 16:23; the peace-sign request; "Black suv"; the arrest.
6. **Each Jane Doe entry is also an account of another attempt to buy sex.** Counsel should weigh that before any of it is disclosed, because it invites questions about every one of them.

### 9.5 "Ok. Where at?"

The exchange, from the UC log:

| Time | Sender | Message |
|---|---|---|
| 15:51:36 | Him | "Can I send a Uber for you?" |
| 15:52:10 | UC | "no im workin wit my girl tonight" |
| 15:52:55 | UC | "we have a room" |
| 15:53:32 | UC | "2 for 1 tonight 100" |
| 15:53:50 | Him | "Ok. Where at?" |
| 15:56:39 | UC | "comfort inn belton" |
| 15:57 | Him | Searches the Belton hotel (Google activity) |
| 15:58:25 | Him | "Its 20 min away from me" |

**The defense reading at its best.** He had asked whether he could send an Uber. She answered no, they had a room. "Ok. Where at?" completes that exchange: fine, where is it? On this reading "Ok" answers her reply to his own question, and the price line is an upsell he did not respond to. That is a coherent reading, and what a short text meant is a question of fact for the panel or jury.

**Why most fact-finders will still read it as going along with the offer**

1. It came 18 seconds after the price, which is long enough to read it. The 2-second gap before "Yes" does real work. This gap does not.
2. He did not question or refuse the price or the second girl, then or later.
3. What followed points the same way: "Just you….then both," which slide 7 says answered "2 for 1 tonight 100"; "Do guys have pictures?"; "Cash right?"; "Ill give extra."
4. He had paid for sex before (PHO finding 4g), so slide 5's claim that "2 for 1 didn't make sense until later" will not be believed.

**What the hotel search adds.** It shows that "where" was a real question about location, which nobody disputes. It does not help decide what "Ok" answered, because on both readings he wanted to know where the room was. It also works for the government: looking up the hotel, saying "Its 20 min away from me" and driving there is the planning of the trip. And it sits badly with slide 7, which says the address is what made him think it was a scam.

**What winning the point would be worth.** Little.

- It would correct PHO finding 4d, that he agreed to pay $100 for both, as to the moment of 15:53:50.
- It would not remove agreement to pay. "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra" show that on their own, and "extra" assumes a price already understood. Spec 3 and the Texas charge do not need "Ok."
- It does not touch Specs 1 and 2, which turn on who he believed she was.
- The reading concedes that he wanted to know where the room was. The next question is "Why?", and the answer collides with "I did not intend to meet anyone."

**How to use it.** As a narrow, accurate correction: the message is ambiguous, and the PHO treated it as a firm agreement to a price. Say it once and move on. It should not be paired with "I didn't understand 2 for 1," and it should not be offered as one of three misreadings (section 8.3, point 7). If the Spec 3 conduct is conceded for credibility (section 5, point 1), this point may not be worth raising at all. He has since paired it with a reading of "100" as a time (section 16.1).

### 9.6 Additions for the forensic examiner, through counsel

1. Which texting apps were used; install, uninstall and login events on both days; whether the app restores history at login; whether the UC thread was deleted, and when.
2. Each grey entry: recipient, time, app, content, and the ad it answered.
3. How the 15:49 message was started: from an ad page, typed, or from an existing thread.
4. The source and precision of the location entries.
5. The texting provider's own records for the account.
6. The arrest time and the vehicle's position, from DPS records and video.

He should keep following counsel's instruction not to reinstall the app or open the account through the cloud. Any change to the account now could be read as tampering. Section 11.1 covers how the records can be obtained without his touching it.

## 10. Addendum 3, 21 Nov 2026: the defense's single account of May 1

**What this covers.** The account supplied after section 9, set out as given and then tested against the texts, the phone records and the interview statement. I have not seen the second photo, the other May 1 conversations or the video.

**The account as given**

1. "Are you still around?" was meant for a woman he had been texting earlier on May 1. It went to the UC's number by accident. He never meant to contact the person from April 30.
2. The reply gave a location different from the one he expected, so he suspected a scam and asked for a picture. He thought the picture was fake. "Im convinced lol" was sarcasm, and he asked for another picture with a peace sign.
3. The second picture resembled the woman on the Twitter profile. He compared it with that profile, or possibly with a picture on the ad site. He did not realize it belonged to the person from the day before.
4. Together, these partly explain why what he said in the interview that evening was inaccurate.

**Short version**

- Settling on one version of how the May 1 message was sent is an improvement. Step 1 is also the most valuable thing the defense could prove, because it would take away the government's best fact, the deliberate return.
- Step 1 has a practical problem. The easiest way to text the wrong person is to open the wrong conversation, and if the UC conversation was open, the April 30 refusal was on the screen.
- Step 3 hurts as stated. He found the Twitter profile only by searching the April 30 person's photo. Checking a May 1 photo against it means he linked the two.
- The account stops at the second photo. It does not say what he believed when he arrived, asked "Cash right?" and offered extra, which is what Specs 1 and 2 turn on.
- The three steps do not explain the interview statement. They describe the opposite of it. What might explain it is what the agents had told him before he made it, and the video will show that.

### 10.1 What the account improves

1. **One version of the re-contact.** The deck had two (section 8.1, point 5). This keeps the one on slide 3, which was already in the deck. Slide 2(e) should be withdrawn.
2. **It can be checked.** The earlier conversation is on the phone or in the provider's records, or it is not. An account that records can prove is worth more than one resting on his word.
3. **It fits his strongest facts.** The one time age came up, he refused within 37 seconds, and the PHO found his history involved adults, "not minors." An accidental contact is consistent with both.
4. **It fits some of the texts.**
   - "Are you still around?" is a natural next line in a conversation from earlier the same afternoon (slide 3 points to one at about 15:18). He used similar lines with other numbers on April 14 and April 25 (slide 4).
   - "Can I send a Uber for you?", sent before any location was given, reads more naturally as the next step in an arrangement already under way than as an opening offer to someone he had turned away the day before.
