2. **Before April 30.** His routine: the app installed for a session and removed after it, photos searched, the same opening lines, a hotel looked up after an exchange. This is group B, and it has to be produced.
3. **May 1.** The same routine, and no mention of age.
4. **The app.** Numbers listed without names, and old conversations back in the list after each reinstall.

**The argument counsel could make from them.** When he knew, he refused. What he was shown next was an adult. The next day nobody mentioned age, and he did what he always did. The government has to prove that he believed she was under 16, and for May 1 its proof is inference. This is the core set out in section 11.5, with the routine added. It is an argument for counsel, not wording for him.

**What it lacks**

1. **A reason why her number got the message.** Without the other conversation, the records show a message to the number of someone he had refused the day before. The routine does not help here, and it may hurt. Section 9.2 noted that two of the people he messaged on May 1 were also contacts from the day before. If going back to the previous day's numbers was part of his round, the prosecutor will say that hers was one of them (section 9.3). That is a different account from a message meant for one particular woman, and only one of them can be what happened. He has since said that a round of messages, or an ad, is the more likely (section 19.2).
2. **His belief.** Records give grounds for an inference and no more. That is true for both sides.
3. **An answer to the interview.** The video is a record too, and it is the government's. Unless he explains it, the statement stands as he made it. What the video shows about the question he was answering, and about "look at my phone," decides how far a case on the records can go (16.5).

**Where his testimony fits**

1. **The more the records carry, the less he has to.** That suits a witness who, as he says, cannot recall every detail without them.
2. **What only he can add** is what he believed, and what he remembers of 15:49 and of the second photo. Section 18 deals with hypnosis as a way of recovering more.
3. **The statements in group D come into the case only if he makes them.** Nothing requires him to offer an explanation for each message. If he testifies he will be asked about them, and section 16.3 sets out the answer that needs no misreading, if it is true.
4. **Whether he testifies is his decision, with counsel** (section 5).

### 17.5 Records to add, through counsel

These add to sections 13.7, 15.5 and 16.7.

1. **His earlier conversations with adults,** from the provider, the extraction or screenshots of the kind on slide 4: the lines he used, set beside those of May 1.
2. **Whether age ever came up in any of them,** and what he did.
3. **The sequence on earlier dates:** install, messages, hotel searches, image searches and removal, each with its time. A habit is shown by the same sequence on several dated occasions.
4. **Earlier image searches,** and what he did when a photo traced to someone other than the person advertising.
5. **Group B as a whole,** produced once and complete.

### 17.6 What this changes

1. **His test is the right one,** and 17.2 applies it. His strongest points pass it without his testimony.
2. **Habit is the best single explanation offered so far for what he did.** It needs records from before April 30, and it concedes that he was arranging to pay.
3. **Three of the answers in section 16 fail the test,** and a fourth depends on a record that may no longer exist. The case does not need them.
4. **Section 1's three points remain.** None is a habit, and two of them depend on records in group C.
5. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third. The records in group B can be gathered now.

## 18. Addendum 11, 23 Nov 2026: hypnosis as a way of recovering memory

**What this covers.** Three questions asked after section 17, recorded as asked. I could not open the decisions cited here. What follows rests on what a web search returns from them, and on the rules as I understand them. Counsel should confirm each point.

1. Can he use a hypnotherapist to recall the events accurately, so that what he recalls can be entered in the record?
2. Will that matter either way if the trial is decided on the evidence?
3. Would the hypnotherapist not be an expert witness?

**Short version**

- He is allowed to. A court may not refuse to hear a defendant only because he has been hypnotized (*Rock v. Arkansas*). It may still examine how the hypnosis was done, and limit or discount what came from it.
- It would not do what he wants. The research finding is consistent. Hypnosis produces more recollections, true and false, and more confidence in both. It does not make recall more accurate. Afterwards nobody can tell the true ones from the false, and that includes him.
- By his own test in section 17, it adds nothing. A detail recovered under hypnosis counts only if a record confirms it, and then the record is the evidence.
- The hypnotherapist could be called, but not for either purpose he has in mind. The hypnotherapist could not repeat what was said in the session as proof that it happened, and could not say that the recall is accurate. All that is left is describing how the session was run. On that, a hypnotherapist chosen and paid by the accused, without a license in psychology or psychiatry, fails the first two things courts look for.
- The cost falls where his case is thinnest. The one thing only he can supply is what he believed on May 1. After hypnosis the government can say that his memory of it was produced in a session, and call an expert who has the research on his side.
- It cannot be undone. He should have no session about these events before counsel has advised. My assessment is that it is more likely to harm his case than to help it. Section 19.4 deals with his reply that the purpose would be to show honesty.

### 18.1 Whether he can

1. **The Supreme Court has ruled on a defendant's own testimony.** In *Rock v. Arkansas*, 483 U.S. 44 (1987), a woman charged with shooting her husband could not remember the details. A licensed neuropsychologist trained in hypnosis hypnotized her twice, and the sessions were recorded. Afterwards she remembered details suggesting that the gun had misfired. An expert then examined the gun and found it defective. The state courts allowed her to testify only to what she had told the doctor before hypnosis. The Supreme Court held that a rule excluding everything a defendant recalls after hypnosis, in every case, violates the right to testify in one's own defense.
2. **The decision has limits.** The Court said that a state may set guidelines for judging such testimony, and may show that the testimony in a particular case is so unreliable that it should be excluded. It described three ways in which hypnosis produces inaccurate memories. The subject becomes suggestible and tries to give answers that will please. He fills gaps from imagination to make the account complete. And he becomes highly confident in true and false memories alike. The Court called the last of these memory hardening, and said it makes cross-examination harder.
3. **What made the difference in *Rock* was the gun.** The detail she recalled could be tested against a physical object, and it held. Section 18.3 comes back to this.
4. **The court-martial.** I found no Military Rule of Evidence on hypnosis. M.R.E. 707 deals only with polygraphs. Where the rules are silent, M.R.E. 101(b) directs courts-martial to the federal rules and the cases applying them, as I understand it. *Rock* is a constitutional ruling, so it applies there too. Three decisions bear on it.
   - *United States v. Scheffer*, 523 U.S. 303 (1998). This was a court-martial case. The Supreme Court upheld M.R.E. 707 and distinguished *Rock*: the rule did not stop the accused from giving his own version of the facts. It barred only expert opinion offered to bolster his credibility. So his own testimony has a protection that an expert called to support his credibility does not.
   - *United States v. Harrington*, 18 M.J. 797 (A.C.M.R. 1984). I could confirm the citation and not the holding. My recollection is that the Army court allowed the testimony of a hypnotized witness where safeguards had been followed. Counsel should read it.
   - *United States v. Mustafa*, 22 M.J. 165 (C.M.A. 1986). I have read only the description in the government's brief opposing Supreme Court review, which was denied (479 U.S. 953). A member of the Air Force agreed to hypnotic interviews with investigators to refresh his memory of what he had seen. Six months later he made statements to them, and those statements were admitted against him at his trial for murder. His defense argued that the hypnosis had led him to believe in events that were not true. The trial judge found the later statements voluntary. According to the brief, the court below also rejected his argument that statements made after hypnosis should be excluded in every case, or admitted only if rigorous safeguards had been used in the session.
5. **Texas.** *Zani v. State*, 758 S.W.2d 233 (Tex. Crim. App. 1988), puts the burden on whoever offers the testimony to show by clear and convincing evidence that it is trustworthy. The court lists what a judge weighs:
   - the hypnotist's training in the clinical and forensic uses of hypnosis;
   - the hypnotist's independence from the police, the prosecution and the defense;
   - a record of what the hypnotist was told about the case beforehand;
   - a written or recorded account of what the subject remembered before hypnosis;
   - recordings of every contact between the two;
   - who else was present, and where the session was held;
   - whether the techniques used were appropriate;
   - whether hypnosis suits the kind of memory loss involved;
   - evidence that corroborates what was recalled.

   That case concerned a prosecution witness. How it applies to a defendant's own testimony after *Rock* is for Texas counsel.
6. **Texas has since moved against the technique.** The Texas Rangers ended their hypnosis program in January 2021. Since 1 September 2023, article 38.24 of the Code of Criminal Procedure has made statements that follow hypnosis by a law enforcement agency inadmissible against a defendant. The statute does not cover a defendant's own hypnosis. It does show how the state now regards hypnotic recall.
7. **The direction elsewhere is the same.** New Jersey wrote the safeguards that many courts borrowed (*State v. Hurd*, 86 N.J. 525 (1981)). In 2006 its Supreme Court abandoned them and held the hypnotically refreshed testimony of a witness generally inadmissible in criminal trials (*State v. Moore*, 188 N.J. 182). That rule concerns witnesses. A defendant's own testimony is still governed by *Rock*.

So the answer to the first question is yes, in this sense: neither forum could refuse to hear him only because he had been hypnotized. As I understand it, each could hold a hearing on how it was done, and each could limit or discount what it produced.

### 18.2 Whether it would make his recall accurate

1. **The research says no.** In 1985 the American Medical Association's Council on Scientific Affairs reviewed the evidence and found that recollections obtained during hypnosis "can involve confabulations and pseudomemories and not only fail to be more accurate, but actually appear to be less reliable than nonhypnotic recall." A 1994 review of 24 studies found that hypnotized subjects reported more false detail and were more confident in what they recalled (Steblay and Bothwell, *Law and Human Behavior*).
2. **Nobody can sort the results afterwards.** The opinion in *Zani* sets out the point. During a session neither the subject nor the hypnotist can tell true memories from false ones, and afterwards neither an expert nor a judge or jury can. Unless a claimed memory is verified by wholly independent means, "no one can reliably tell whether it is an accurate recollection or mere confabulation."
3. **What he would get is confidence.** He would come out of a session surer of more things. He would not come out more accurate, and he would have no way of knowing which of the new things were true.
4. **His situation is close to the worst case for it.**
   - Seven months have passed.
   - He knows exactly which gaps matter and what would help in each: what was on the screen at 15:49, whether he deleted her conversation, why he searched her photo, and whom "Cash right?" was for.
   - He has a detailed account of the afternoon, built since the arrest from records and reasoning (sections 10 to 17). A hypnotized subject fills gaps with what he expects and with what makes the account complete. For him that material is ready to hand. What is most likely to come back is the account in this document, now felt as a memory.
5. **Some of what he wants was never stored.** On his own account he did not notice whose number he was texting, and he does not recall seeing the April 30 messages. Section 17.1 said that routine acts are not remembered one by one. If that is right, there is nothing there to recover. Whatever a session produced on those points would be new.
6. **A belief is not an event.** The question in Specs 1 and 2 is what he believed on May 1. Hypnosis cannot retrieve a belief more accurately than he can state it now. It can only make him more certain of whatever he says.

### 18.3 Whether it would matter if the trial is decided on the records

1. **His own test answers this.** Section 17 accepted his point that what he says matters where records support it. Apply that to a detail recovered under hypnosis.

   | The recovered detail | What it is worth |
   |---|---|
   | A record confirms it | The record is the evidence. It could have been looked for without the session |
   | A record contradicts it | It is one more version, and he now holds it with more confidence than before |
   | No record either way | It rests on his word, and on a method the research does not support |

2. ***Rock* is an example of the first row.** Her recollection mattered because the gun could be examined. The things that can be examined here are already listed in groups B and C of section 17.2: his Google activity, the provider's records, the extraction and the video. Each can be sought directly.
3. **The one real use of hypnosis is as a lead.** If a session pointed to a record nobody had thought to look for, the record would stand on its own, however it was found. I cannot identify such a record here. If counsel can, that is the case for considering it, and it would have to be done through counsel, with the safeguards listed in 18.1 and 18.4.
4. **A case that rests on the records needs him for one thing.** Section 17.4 said the records can carry what he did. What only he can add is what he believed. That is the subject on which hypnosis is weakest, and on which a doubt about his memory costs most.
5. **How a recollection gets into the record.** On the question of guilt, only through his sworn testimony, subject to cross-examination. A written account, a recording of a session or a hypnotist's report cannot be offered by the defense as proof of what happened. His own out-of-court statements are hearsay when he is the one offering them. The exception for a recorded recollection needs a record made while the matter was fresh (M.R.E. 803(5)), which a session seven months later is not. An unsworn statement is available at sentencing only (R.C.M. 1001).

### 18.4 Whether the hypnotherapist would be an expert witness

The hypnotherapist could be called. What matters is what the testimony would be allowed to cover.

| What the hypnotist would say | Allowed? |
|---|---|
| What he said during the session | Not as proof that it happened. His own earlier statements are hearsay when the defense offers them. One survey of the cases reports that courts "have uniformly rejected the admissibility of out-of-court statements made while an individual is in hypnosis." *Greenfield v. Commonwealth*, 214 Va. 710 (1974), is an example |
| That his recall is accurate, or that he was telling the truth | No. Nothing can separate a true hypnotic memory from a false one (18.2). Military courts also bar what they call human lie detector testimony, an opinion that a person was truthful about a fact in issue (*United States v. Knapp*, 73 M.J. 33 (C.A.A.F. 2014)). *Scheffer* (18.1) treats expert opinion offered to bolster an accused's credibility as something a rule may exclude |
| How the session was conducted | Yes, to the judge, in deciding whether his testimony is reliable enough to be heard. This is where the hypnotist's own training and independence are examined |

1. **An expert has to be qualified, and the method has to be reliable** (M.R.E. 702). The government would call its own expert, probably a research psychologist, and the research is on that side (18.2).
2. **Courts look for a particular kind of hypnotist.** The safeguards in *Hurd* and the factors in *Zani* start with the same two things: a psychiatrist or psychologist trained in hypnosis, who is independent of both sides. In *Rock* the hypnotist was a licensed neuropsychologist.
3. **"Hypnotherapist" is often not a licensed profession.** In Texas the statutory definition of the practice of psychology includes hypnosis (Occupations Code § 501.003), and a 1995 Attorney General opinion concluded that hypnotherapy for pay required a psychology license (DM-0321). A hypnotist outside those professions may hold no license at all. A federal appeals court upheld the exclusion of a plaintiff's testimony about memories that followed hypnosis. The hypnotist had no formal training in psychiatry or psychotherapy and, as I read the summaries, no record of the sessions had been kept (*Borawick v. Shay*, 68 F.3d 597 (2d Cir. 1995), a civil case).
4. **A hypnotist he hires is not independent.** That is the second safeguard.
5. **Privilege.** The military psychotherapist privilege covers a psychiatrist, clinical psychologist, clinical social worker or other licensed mental health professional, and communications made for the diagnosis or treatment of a mental or emotional condition (M.R.E. 513). A session with a lay hypnotherapist, held to recover memory for a trial, is probably neither. An expert engaged by counsel as part of the defense team is in a different position (M.R.E. 502; defense motions cite *United States v. Toledo*, 25 M.J. 270 (C.M.A. 1987), for this). Even then, if he testifies to what the session produced, the recordings are what the judge and the other side will examine. Section 7, point 8, notes that what goes to a testifying expert can reach the prosecutor.
6. **What he says around a session is less protected than he might expect.** Courts have refused statements made in the hypnotic state from either side, including confessions. But a session run properly also records what he says before and after it, and those are ordinary statements. Anything in them that helps him cannot be offered by him. Anything that hurts can be offered against him as his own statement, if the government obtains the recording (M.R.E. 801(d)(2)). *Mustafa* (18.1) is a case in which statements an accused made after hypnosis were used at his trial, and in which, on the brief's account, the court did not require that the session had met safeguards.

### 18.5 What it would cost

1. **His testimony on belief would carry a label.** Everything he says about May 1 after a session can be described as hypnotically refreshed. The judge may hold a hearing on it first. The members or jurors would learn of the hypnosis in cross-examination, and could hear the government's expert explain what it does to memory.
2. **There is a record of what he remembered before.** Where a court limits testimony after hypnosis, the usual line is what the person remembered before the session. For him that is on record: the interview that evening, the deck, and the points in this document on which he has said he does not recall (sections 13.4, 13.6 and 15). A memory of exactly those points, appearing after hypnosis, invites the comparison.
3. **Memory hardening works against him as a witness.** Section 13.6 said that "I don't recall" is the safe answer where the records may go against him. After hypnosis he may no longer be able to give it honestly, because he will feel that he does recall. If a record then contradicts him, he will have been confidently wrong under oath.
4. **It adds a version.** Sections 8.3, 13.6 and 15.4 count the points that have been given more than one way. An account given after hypnosis would be another, and the easiest one to attack.
5. **It cannot be undone.** Once he has been hypnotized about these events, every later statement comes after it. He could not then choose to leave the hypnotic material out of his testimony, because he would not be able to separate it from the rest.
6. **The recordings exist.** A session done properly is recorded from start to finish. A session done without recordings fails the safeguards (18.4).

### 18.6 What could be done instead

1. **Counsel takes down his present memory now, in full.** It is privileged. It fixes what he remembered unaided, before records, discussion or anything else changes it further. It is also the first thing a court would ask for if hypnosis were ever used.
2. **The records.** Looking at a record to refresh memory is the ordinary, accepted method (section 16.6, on M.R.E. 612). Groups B and C of section 17.2 are the list.
3. **A psychologist engaged through counsel, for one of two purposes.**
   - *To explain memory to the fact-finder.* Why routine acts are not remembered one by one, and how a person keeps a fact and loses where it came from (section 11.2). That is expert evidence that fits his case, if counsel thinks the judge would admit it. It supports "I don't recall." It does not replace it.
   - *To interview him without hypnosis.* The best-studied method is the cognitive interview. A 2010 review of the research found a large increase in correct details and a small increase in errors, compared with an ordinary interview (Memon, Meissner and Fraser, *Psychology, Public Policy, and Law*). The same review found that the benefit shrinks, and invented detail grows, as the time since the event lengthens. At seven months the gain may be modest. Whether to do it is for counsel.
4. **Treatment is a separate matter.** Seeing a licensed clinician about the behavior he describes as out of control is worth doing for its own sake, and it bears on negotiation and sentence (section 7, point 7). Counsel should know before it starts, because the privilege has exceptions. If the clinician proposes hypnosis as part of treatment, counsel should be asked first. Hypnosis given as therapy can be raised against his testimony just as a memory session can, if it touches on the events. *Borawick* arose from hypnosis given as therapy.

### 18.7 What this changes

1. **Nothing in the assessment of the charges.**
2. **One addition to section 7.** No hypnosis about these events before counsel has advised. If a session has already taken place, counsel should be told now, and any recording or notes should be kept.
3. **The three questions, answered.** He may. It would not make his memory more accurate. It would matter only where a record confirmed it, and the records can be sought without it.
4. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third.

## 19. Addendum 12, 24 Nov 2026: four objections to this analysis

**What this covers.** Four objections supplied after section 18, recorded as relayed, with the new details they contain. I have not seen the charge sheet, the full log, the location data or the video, and I no longer have the deck to check against.

1. The statement that a point "can be made from the exhibits alone" is naive. Several officers had the log with its timestamps and overlooked or disregarded them. A reading of one fragment of text stayed central to a specification, which alleges a wish to ejaculate on a minor. It stood for months, until his defense counsel gave him the log and he pointed out the error himself.
2. The analysis tries to narrow each situation to one answer, when more than one can be true. He says this is also the core reason he could not give a clear account in the interview. Within this objection:
   - He drove to the hotel compulsively, without a good reason or against reason, while suspecting a scam. The suspicion came from an earlier experience at the same location.
   - The recipients were unknown to him. So "she was the woman on the profile, an adult" is misleading. It is more likely that he saw the ad and sent a message, or messaged existing conversations together, starting with those most recently used.
   - Geolocation will show his movements while the texts were sent.
   - His answer on "2 for 1 tonight 100" does not change his earlier one. It emphasizes that the offer was not overtly accepted.
3. On "giving money and leaving was never his plan": there was never a plan. If there had been one, a man who had used escorts for years would probably not have been caught.
4. The purpose of hypnosis would be to convey honesty, whether or not it helps, because imperfect recall, or "I don't remember," hurts credibility and can look like deceit.

**Short version**

- The first objection is right, and three passages have been changed. An exhibit makes a point only when someone presses it. The example he gives also deserves more weight than this analysis gave it. If "Yes" was sent before her second line could be read, then on my understanding that part of Spec 1 fails, whatever he believed about her age. It removes one message. "Just you….then both" is still there.
- The second objection is right about states of mind. One attribution in section 11 was my inference, and it is withdrawn. Put in his own terms, his account is one description with several parts. The parts fit each other, and three of the collisions listed in section 8.3 fall away.
- It is not right about facts. Only one thing happened at 15:49, and he now says he does not know what. The first step of the account has moved from a wrong recipient to an unknown one. That is more candid and needs fewer assumptions. It also gives up the adult profile as the ground of what he believed.
- One question still needs one answer: whether he knew whose number it was. Specs 1 and 2 turn on it. Everything else can stay mixed.
- The third objection is right about the word. But an attempt needs an intent, not a plan. An intent can form in a moment and can depend on a condition. The argument that an experienced man would not have been caught has a useful core for counsel and a costly form for him.
- On the fourth, the purpose is understood. Hypnosis would show that he is sincere, which nobody would doubt. It would not show that he is accurate, and a sincere witness who is contradicted by a record looks dishonest. Other things show honesty better, and he has been doing several of them.

### 19.1 Exhibits do not speak for themselves

**The correction.** He is right. Section 13.4 said that a point could be made "from the exhibits alone." Section 16.1 said that counsel could make a point from the log "without his testimony." Section 17.2 said that his strongest points did not depend on his memory and could be shown through the exhibits. Each sentence described what is possible. None said who would do it, and his experience is that nobody did. The passages have been changed.

**The example, as relayed.** Spec 1 alleges that he communicated a wish to ejaculate on the person he was texting. The words come from this exchange.

| Time on May 1 | From | Message |
|---|---|---|
| 15:58:25 | Him | "Its 20 min away from me" |
| 15:58:57 | Undercover | "ok u gonna cum" |
| 15:59:19 | Undercover | "on both of us lol" |
| 15:59:21 | Him | "Yes" |
| 16:00:22 | Him | "Just you….then both" |

As relayed, several officers had this log, and the reading that he had said yes to the second line stood for months. It was questioned only when his counsel gave him the log and he saw the two seconds. I do not know whether the specification has been changed since.

**Why it deserves more weight than this analysis gave it**

1. **It answers that part of Spec 1 without any reference to her age.** Section 2 listed the timing as a weakness in the government's case, and section 16.1 called it his strongest point on a single message. Both treated it as a doubt about what he meant. It is more than that. The act the specification is built on is "intentionally communicating indecent language" (Article 120b(h)(5)(C)). If "Yes" answered the first line, read as a question about whether he was coming, then he did not communicate the second line at all. Every other point on Specs 1 and 2 depends on what he believed about her. This one does not.
2. **Two seconds probably overstates the time he had.** If the log is from the undercover phone, it shows when her messages left and when his arrived. Her second line had to reach his phone, and his "Yes" had to travel back, inside those two seconds. The examiner can say what each timestamp records and how long delivery takes. If the provider's records survive, they will show when his app received her line and when it sent his.
3. **It does not need his testimony.** The inference comes from the order of the messages: his line about the distance, her question, his answer. Counsel can draw it out of the agent with the log in hand.

**What it does not remove**

1. **"Just you….then both."** It was sent a minute later, when her second line had been in front of him for some time. The government will read it as his answer to "on both of us." He says it answered the offer of two women, which he took her to be repeating (section 16.1). On either reading he wrote it after seeing her line.
2. **"ok u gonna cum" taken alone.** The agent spelled it the sexual way, and the government may say that "Yes" agreed to that line by itself. The answer is the message about distance just before it, and the way people spell in texts.
3. **The message about the car at 16:58,** which the hearing officer also relied on for Spec 1 (section 8.2).
4. **Short replies can carry this charge.** Words that are chaste one by one can be indecent as a whole, particularly when said to a child. In *United States v. French*, 31 M.J. 57 (C.M.A. 1990), asking a stepdaughter under 16 whether he could climb into bed with her was enough. The court there worked from the principle that where language is not indecent on its face, the specification itself has to make the indecency clear. So the exact words of Spec 1 matter, and counsel should read them against the log line by line.

**The same thing has happened at other points**

| What the government's papers say | What the exhibit shows | Section |
|---|---|---|
| He said yes to "on both of us" | "Yes" arrived two seconds after that line | 2 |
| He agreed at 15:53:50 to pay $100 for both | "Ok. Where at?" followed his question about a car and her answer about a room. Nothing in it accepts a price | 9.5 |
| The $50 agreed on April 30 counts toward his intent | It was agreed before she gave her age. He refused 37 seconds after she gave it | 2 |
| He "hesitated" | He refused in 37 seconds and told her to change her life | 2 |
| He answered an ad for an underage child | No ad was in the packet, and the hearing officer rejected the claim | 2 |
| He "admitted to knowingly speaking to a minor" | The officer who watched the video did not cite it for that | 2 |
| The Texas draft cites § 43.031(b)(2) | That looks like the wrong section for a buyer | 4 |

1. **Each is small. Together they are a theme for counsel.** The government's account was built from summaries, and at most of these points the summary says more than the exhibit does. The remedy is the same every time: the fact-finder reads the log itself, with the times.
2. **Only one was caught inside the government's own process.** The hearing officer rejected the claim about the ad. The rest were found by reading the exhibits against the summaries.

**What follows**

1. **For the person deciding, a point from an exhibit exists only when someone puts it in front of him.** It may have to be put again at each stage. Nothing obliges a reader to notice a timestamp.
2. **His reading of the records has value, and this is the proof.** The place for what he finds is a note to counsel, which is privileged.
3. **The form that works is one page for each point:** the exhibit, the lines, the times, and what the government's papers say instead. The table above is a start.
4. **Ways of pressing the timing, each for counsel to judge.** These are my understanding of the rules.
   - In writing, to whoever decides what goes to trial. For offenses like these that is, as I understand it, a special trial counsel and not the commander (Articles 1(17) and 24a, UCMJ). Before referral a specification can be changed. After it, a major change cannot be made over his objection unless the charge is withdrawn and referred again (R.C.M. 603).
   - A bill of particulars, to make the government say which messages it relies on for Spec 1 (R.C.M. 906(b)(6)).
   - Cross-examination of the agent who wrote the messages: the five lines, the five times, and why the question was sent in two parts.
   - The examiner, on what each timestamp records.
   - A motion for a finding of not guilty as to that language, when the government has finished its case (R.C.M. 917). The standard favors the government. The motion fails if any evidence could reasonably tend to establish each element.
   - Findings by exceptions. A fact-finder may find a specification proved with some of its words left out, so long as that does not change the nature of the offense (R.C.M. 918(a)(1)).
5. **None of this requires him to testify.** He found the point. Counsel makes it. The log proves it.

### 19.2 More than one thing true at once

**What is right in the objection**

1. **A state of mind is often several things at once.** A man can suspect a scam, go anyway, and have decided nothing about what he will do when he arrives.
2. **Several tables in this document gave each moment one belief** (sections 10.4, 13.6, 16.2 and 16.4). That is how a prosecutor sets out a case and how an interviewer asks questions. It is not what the law requires of him. The government has to prove one state of mind beyond a reasonable doubt: for Specs 1 and 2, that he believed she was under 16. He does not have to prove a different one in its place. A state of mind in which he had no clear idea who it was, and no thought of a minor, is a complete answer to those charges if it leaves a reasonable doubt.
3. **A demand for one reason can produce a wrong one.** Section 13.2 cited the research. Asked why they did something, people give a tidy reason and do not notice that they are not remembering. His objection applies that to the interview, and it is fair (see "The interview" below).

**The correction.** Section 11.2 said that from the second photo he "took the person he was texting to be the woman on the profile, an adult." He did not say that. What he said was that the second photo resembled the profile, or possibly the ad, and that he did not connect it with the day before (section 10). The belief was my inference. Sections 13.6 and 16.4 then set it against his other statements as if it were his. It is withdrawn, and those passages have been changed.

**His state of mind, as he now describes it**

1. There was no plan.
2. The recipients were unknown to him. They were numbers from adult ads, and he did not keep track of which was which.
3. He suspected a scam, because of an earlier experience at the same hotel.
4. He went anyway. He describes this as compulsive: driving there without a good reason, or against reason.
5. At no time did he think he was dealing with a minor.

**These five fit together.** Section 8.3 counted six answers in the deck and nine collisions among them. Three of the nine, numbers 1, 3 and 5, were collisions between a belief founded on the profile and the other answers. With the correction they go. Number 2, the two versions of how the message was sent, becomes a question for the records. What is left is one description with five parts. That is what section 8.3 asked for. It asked for one account, not one motive.

**What can be true together, and what cannot**

| Pair | Together? |
|---|---|
| Suspecting a scam, and going anyway | Yes. This is what he calls compulsion |
| No plan, and an intent formed on the spot | Yes (19.3) |
| Not knowing who she was, and taking her for an adult | Yes. It is what he took all of them for |
| Nervous about a scam, and nervous because buying sex is illegal | Yes |
| The offer of extra as a test, and as an offer he might have kept | Yes. A test and an offer do not exclude each other |
| No intention to meet, and the messages at the hotel | Yes, if it means that he had decided nothing. No, if it means that he had decided not to |
| "100" taken as a price, and taken as a time | No. A third possibility is that he did not take the line in |
| "Cash right?" written to her, and meant for someone else | No. A fact |
| Her conversation opened from the list, and already deleted | No. A fact (section 15.2) |
| Not knowing whose number it was, and knowing | No. This is the one that decides Specs 1 and 2 |

1. **An explanation that sits beside the natural meaning of a message costs little. One that replaces it costs a great deal.** Section 16.2 counted ten messages and acts that had been given another meaning. Several belong to the first kind. Two more are supported by the log itself: the timing of "Yes," and the request for another photo that followed "Im convinced lol." So the count overstated the problem. The second kind, with nothing in the records behind it, is a short list: "100" as a time, "Cash right?" as meant for someone else, and the extra as nothing but a test.
2. **A mixture is not the same as a change.** A mixture is the same description every time it is given. What a cross-examiner uses is a different answer on a different day (section 13.6). If the truth has five parts, each telling has to have all five.
3. **Where he does not know which of two facts happened, "I do not know" is the accurate answer.** "Both" is not available for a fact.

**What takes the profile's place.** On this account he held no particular belief about her age on May 1. He assumed what he assumed about every number he texted: an adult. The ads are for adults, the pictures were of an adult, and the one person who had said otherwise he had turned away. That does not need a split memory as the ground of a belief, which eases the difficulty set out in section 11.2. The difficulty does not disappear, because he has said that the second photo resembled the profile (point 6 under "The first message" below). And the assumption rests entirely on his not knowing whose number it was. The profile stays in the case as evidence that everything he was shown was an adult, and as counsel's argument in the alternative (section 11.3).

**The first message**

*As relayed: it is more likely that he saw the ad and sent a message, or messaged existing conversations together, starting with the most recent.*

1. **This is a judgment about what is likely. It is not a memory.** It belongs in the third column of the first table in section 13.6.
2. **It differs from sections 10 and 12,** which had a particular woman from earlier that afternoon and a wrong conversation opened by mistake. All the versions share one thing: he did not know whose number it was, and did not mean to reach the person from April 30. That is the first step of the account. The mechanism is for the records.
3. **There are now four ways it could have happened.**

   | How | What was in front of him | Record that would show it |
   |---|---|---|
   | He meant another woman and opened the wrong conversation (section 12.2) | Her conversation, with the April 30 lines | The other woman's conversation |
   | Her conversation had been deleted, and he entered the number again (section 15.2) | An empty conversation. The number came from somewhere, most likely her ad | A deletion, and how the number was entered |
   | He saw her ad and answered it | The ad, with the photo he had searched the day before. Then her conversation, if it still existed | The page open before 15:49 |
   | He sent the same line to several recent conversations | Each conversation in turn, hers among them | The same line to other numbers within minutes |

4. **The fourth has something in the timeline.**
   - If the timeline's entries are right, he had messaged five numbers that day before hers. In a list sorted by most recent use, as most apps sort them, her conversation would have come next. It was last used the afternoon before, so it sat directly below those five. A man working down the list would have reached it sixth.
   - That would give an innocent reading to something section 9.3 counted against him, that she was contacted last.
   - The timeline has him stopped for gas and food from 15:45 to 15:50, and slide 4 lists a message to another number at 15:45. A round of messages sent during that stop would look like this.
   - The entries for the other five are assumptions, and at least one message that day went from the phone's ordinary messaging app (section 9.4). The examiner can list everything sent between about 15:40 and 15:55, and the order of the list.
5. **What the change gains.** It no longer needs a particular other woman, whose conversation may not exist any more (section 14.2). It fits the stock line and the habit (section 17.3). It is candid about what he does not know. It is close to the first answer in section 6, question 2.
6. **What it costs.**
   - In each of the four ways, something of hers was on the screen: her conversation, with the April 30 lines, or her ad, with the photo he had searched.
   - It is another version of the first step (section 13.6). The defense should now say one thing: he does not remember how, and the records will show.
   - It does not sit easily with section 13.5. A man who picks out her conversation in order to block it knows which one it is. "The recipients were unknown" fits that only if he meant to block her on April 30 and not on the afternoon of May 1. He gave no time.
   - The second photo. He has said that it resembled the profile, or possibly the ad (section 10). If he knew the face from the profile, he had connected the person texting with the ad he had searched. On his present account he concluded nothing from that. What he compared it with is a fact. Unless he remembers it, it belongs in the third column too.

**Compulsion**

1. **What he describes.** Driving to a hotel without a good reason, or against reason, while expecting a scam. He compares it with obsessive-compulsive behavior. The World Health Organization's description of compulsive sexual behaviour disorder includes going on with the behavior "despite adverse consequences or deriving little or no satisfaction from it." Whether that or any diagnosis fits him is for an evaluator (section 17.3).
2. **What it can do on the question of guilt.** The government reasons from what he did to what he thought: he drove there, so he believed she was real and meant to go through with it. Compulsion answers that step. If he goes whether or not it makes sense, the fact that he went shows less.
3. **What it cannot do.** A man acting on a compulsion still means to do each thing he does. It does not remove intent, and for Spec 3 it is close to an admission. It is also the government's explanation: the urge that took him back to someone he had refused (section 3). It helps him only if he did not know whose number it was.
4. **It needs more than his word.** An evaluation, and records of earlier trips that ended in nothing.
5. **Offering it at trial has consequences.** As I understand the rules, a mental condition short of a lack of mental responsibility is not a defense, but evidence of it may be admitted on whether he had the state of mind an offense requires. The defense has to give notice before trial of expert testimony on his mental condition (R.C.M. 701(b)(2) and the discussion to R.C.M. 916(k)(2)). Once he offers evidence of his own mental condition, the privilege for what he has told a therapist is subject to an exception (M.R.E. 513(d)(7)), and the government may ask for its own examination. These are counsel's decisions.

**The earlier scam at the same hotel**

1. **It is new to this analysis.** I had recorded only that the address made him suspect a scam (slide 7, as I noted it).
2. **It fits the log.** "Lol ok." was sent eleven seconds after she named the hotel. He tried the car park next door, asked to be walked upstairs, and then asked her to come to the car.
3. **It can be checked:** messages, a payment and location data from the earlier occasion.
4. **It dates the suspicion.** It began when she named the hotel, at 15:56:39. The messages before that were sent without it.
5. **It costs something.** It is another occasion (section 14.3, point 8). It sharpens the question of why he went, to which compulsion is his answer. And slide 8 describes his search that afternoon as one for signs of a sting. A sting is the police, and a scam is not (section 10.3).

**Location data**

*As relayed: geolocation will show his movements while the texts were sent.*

1. **What it could show for him.**
   - That the replies between about 15:51 and 16:02 were sent from a moving car. A man who is driving reads little and types short answers. That fits the timing of "Yes," and it fits not taking in a price.
   - That the afternoon was errands and several contacts, with no movement toward Belton before 15:58.
   - That he hesitated at the hotel: the car park next door, time spent sitting, no approach to the door.
   - Earlier trips, to the same hotel or to others, that ended with nothing.
2. **What it could show against him.**
   - The moment he turned toward Belton, and that the stop in Harker Heights lay on the way.
   - Fourteen minutes at the hotel, from 16:48 to 17:02, without leaving.
   - Texting while driving is itself an offense in Texas unless the vehicle is stopped (Transportation Code § 545.4251). It is minor beside these charges, but counsel should know before the point is made. A prosecutor may also say that it shows how keen he was.
3. **Whether it still exists.** Google now keeps a phone's location timeline on the phone and not in the account, and its default setting deletes entries after three months. So data from May may survive only on the phone he had then, or in a backup. If the government took that phone on May 1, the data is in its hands. The entries marked "@" on his timeline came from some record, and counsel should know which. Other sources need counsel: the government's extraction, the carrier's records, and the operation's own surveillance at the hotel. Nothing on his side should be changed.

**"2 for 1 tonight 100."** Accepted. The table in section 13.6 listed his answer as a change from slide 5, and it has been corrected. The position is the same in both: the offer was not overtly accepted. The log supports that. "Ok. Where at?" followed her answer about a room, and the finding that he agreed then to pay $100 for both says more than the log does (19.1). The reading of "100" as a time is an addition, and the point does not need it (section 16.1).

**The interview**

1. **His explanation is better than "poor recall."** The questions asked for one reason and one sequence. What he had was several things at once, and no clear idea which number was which. He gave a tidy version. The video will show the form of the questions.
2. **It does not explain one feature of the statement.** As relayed, the tidy version has her as one person on both days: "I refused and thought the next day she just needed money." If the recipients were unknown to him, that had to come from somewhere. Either the agents had told him by then whose number it was, or he knew. The video will show which (section 10.5).

**Where one answer is still needed.** For Specs 1 and 2, on one question: whether he knew whose number it was. Everything else in his account can stay as mixed as he says it was.

### 19.3 "There was never a plan"

**The correction.** "Plan" was my word. The statement as relayed is "I thought about just giving money and leaving," which reports a thought. The sentence in section 16.1 has been changed. Section 9.3 also called the afternoon a deliberate trip. The gas stop began before the first message to her, and the timeline shows errands and other contacts until she named the hotel. Nothing on it points toward Belton before then.

**No plan is not the same as no intent**

1. **An attempt needs an intent and an act. It does not need a plan.** The act has to be done with the specific intent to commit the offense, and has to go beyond preparation (Article 80). Nothing requires forethought. An intent can form in a moment.
2. **An intent can depend on a condition.** The Supreme Court has held that an intent to do something if a condition is met is still an intent (*Holloway v. United States*, 526 U.S. 1 (1999), on a federal statute). The Model Penal Code states the same rule (§ 2.02(6)). Whether a court-martial would apply it to an attempt is for counsel. If it would, "I had no plan" and "I expected a scam" do not answer Spec 3. A state of mind in which he would probably have gone ahead, if she had turned out to be real, is enough.
3. **For Specs 1 and 2 the condition changes nothing.** Whatever he would have done if she was real, the question is still who he believed she was.

**What "no plan" does help with**

1. **The return.** The government's account is that he decided to go back to her. If nothing that afternoon was decided in advance, the message to her number was not a decision either. This is the point about habit in section 17.3.
2. **Resolve.** The standard description of a substantial step is an act that strongly corroborates the intent and shows resolve to commit the offense. Evidence that he was not resolved goes to that: the car park next door, sitting in the car, asking her to come down, removing the app. The government reads the same facts as caution. The location data and the arrest record matter here. Section 20.3 gives the authorities.

**"A man with years of experience would not have been caught"**

1. **There is a usable inference in it, and it belongs to counsel.** A statement that the person is under age is the plainest warning a buyer can get. He stopped at once when he got it. The records and the deck show a man who checks photos and looks for signs of a sting. It is hard to see that man going back, knowingly, to the one number that had given him the warning. It is easier to see him doing it without knowing.
2. **Put the way he puts it, it costs more than it gains.**
   - It is a claim about what would have happened, and nobody can test it.
   - It presents him as practiced at not being caught. The prosecutor will agree, and will point to the second-number apps, the sting search, the photo check, the car park next door and the tinted windows. He did take care, and it was not enough.
   - It pulls against compulsion. If the urge takes him to a hotel against reason, it could have taken him back to her against reason. That is the government's case.
   - "For years" is a statement about other offenses (section 7, point 5).
3. **So the inference should come from the records, through counsel,** and not from him as a statement about himself.

### 19.4 Hypnosis as a way of showing honesty

**The purpose, as relayed.** Not better evidence. He wants to show that he is being honest, whether or not what comes back helps him, because "I don't remember" hurts credibility and can look like deceit.

1. **The concern is real.** An accused who answers "I don't remember" to the questions that matter is often not believed.
2. **Hypnosis shows the wrong thing.** Nobody doubts that a hypnotized person is sincere. The research finding is that he is sincere about things that did not happen (section 18.2). What comes into question afterwards is his accuracy, not his honesty. A session would leave him more sincere and possibly less truthful.
3. **A sincere witness who is contradicted by a record looks dishonest.** That is the result he wants to avoid, and it is the main risk section 18.5 described.
4. **The people who decide would not take it as a sign of honesty.**
   - The fact-finder would probably not hear it from the defense. Evidence that a witness went through a procedure in order to be believed is offered to bolster him. The nearest rule keeps out "any reference to an offer to take, failure to take, or taking of a polygraph examination" (M.R.E. 707). Hypnosis is not in that rule. The comparison is mine, and counsel should confirm it.
   - If the fact-finder heard of it, it would be from the prosecutor, with an expert explaining what hypnosis does to memory. It would then look like the opposite of what he intends: a way of acquiring a memory.
   - Prosecutors weighing an offer would read it the same way.
5. **The order of events would work against him.** He has said that he does not recall what was on the screen, or whether he deleted the conversation (sections 13.4 and 15.1). A memory of those same points, arriving after a session, would be called convenient.

**When "I don't remember" costs credibility, and when it does not**

| It costs | It does not |
|---|---|
| It comes only where the answer would hurt | It comes on points that would help as well |
| It is new, after an account was given at first | It has been the answer from the start |
| The thing is one that people remember | The thing is routine |
| It is followed by an explanation anyway | It is given, and he stops |

1. **The first row is the risk in section 13.6.** His statement that he does not know how the message was sent is on the right side of it.
2. **The second row is the interview.** He gave an account that evening. So "I don't remember" now has to come with the reason that account was given (19.2).
3. **The third row cuts both ways for him.** Which number he texted on an ordinary afternoon is routine. The exchange on April 30 was not.
4. **The fourth row is section 15.4.**

**What does show honesty**

1. **Saying what costs him.** He has done this: that he paid adults for sex for years, that he felt out of control, that he meant to block her number, and now that he does not know how the message came to be sent. Each makes the rest easier to believe.
2. **The same account each time,** including the same "I do not know."
3. **Records produced whole,** including the parts that do not help (section 17.2).
4. **An expert on memory, through counsel,** who can explain to the fact-finder why routine acts are not remembered. That supports "I don't remember" without anyone vouching for him (section 18.6).
5. **For the people who decide before trial, a polygraph arranged by counsel.** Defense counsel sometimes use a favorable result in negotiation. It is not admissible at trial, and the method has reliability problems of its own. Unlike hypnosis it does not change what he remembers. As I understand it, an unfavorable result obtained through counsel stays with counsel. The DPS interview already included a polygraph, and counsel will know what it showed. Whether to do this is for counsel.
6. **He does not have to prove that he is honest.** The burden is the government's. If he does not testify, "I don't remember" is never said, and the members can be told to draw no adverse inference from that (M.R.E. 301(f)(3)). The decision is his, with counsel (section 5).

### 19.5 Records to add, through counsel

These add to sections 13.7, 15.5, 16.7 and 17.5.

1. **The charge sheet as it now stands:** the exact words of Spec 1, and which messages each part rests on.
2. **The timestamps in the undercover log:** what each records, and the provider's own times for the five messages in 19.1 if they survive.
3. **Everything he sent between about 15:40 and 15:55 on May 1,** to any number and from any app, with the words.
4. **The order of the conversation list at 15:45,** and what each entry showed.
5. **Whether her ad was open on his phone before 15:49,** and the ad itself.
6. **Location data for April 30 and May 1:** its source, whether it still exists, and a copy made without changing anything.
7. **The earlier occasion at the same hotel:** messages, any payment, and where he went.
8. **Earlier trips that ended without a meeting.**
9. **The video:** the form of the questions, and what he had been told about the number before the "needed money" statement.

### 19.6 What this changes

1. **Three corrections and one clarification are accepted.** Exhibits need someone to press them. The belief about the profile was my inference. "Plan" was my word. His answer on "2 for 1" was not a change.
2. **The timing of "Yes" moves up.** It answers part of Spec 1 without any reference to her age, and section 1 now says so.
3. **His account is restated in his own terms in section 1.** It is one description with several parts. The parts fit each other, and it needs fewer special readings than the versions before it.
4. **Its first step is now that he did not know whose number it was.** No particular mistake is claimed. That is more candid. It is also a change, and it gives up the profile as the ground of what he believed.
5. **Only one question needs a single answer,** and it is the one in point 4.
6. **The assessment of the charges is unchanged.** Spec 3 is strong, and his account concedes most of it. Specs 1 and 2 turn on what he believed.
7. **On hypnosis the assessment in section 18 stands.**
8. **The order of work is unchanged.** Video first, forensic report second, account third.

## 20. Addendum 13, 24 Nov 2026: nervousness at the hotel, and what it shows about intent

**What this covers.** A fifth point, supplied after section 19 and recorded as relayed. I have not seen the full log, the arrest record or the video. The cases cited here were found by search and read in summaries and extracts, not in full. Each is for counsel to check before it is relied on.

5. On intent. The room was proposed by the undercover agent, early in the May 1 exchange. If the "plan" had stayed the same, why would he suddenly become nervous after driving there, when there was an "agreement on solicitation"? His explanation is that the premise was not genuine, or that he thought the arrangement was a scam, as he has said. He asks for references to any earlier case in which an accused drove to the location and then said he was nervous, on being given the room number or on arriving.

I read "the premise" as the agreement the government says was reached at 15:53:50.

**Short version**

- The facts in the point are right, and the log supports them better than the point does. The room was her proposal. He was wary from the moment she named the hotel, almost an hour before he said he was nervous. Nothing I have seen shows him going to the room.
- The conclusion does not follow as he puts it. A man who says he is nervous expects to meet someone. The message does not show that nothing had been agreed, or that he would not have gone ahead. What he did next was ask her to come to his car, for more money.
- I found no reported case that matches his facts, and none in which nervousness on arrival was accepted as showing that the accused lacked intent. In the cases I found where a court discussed hesitation in a sting, it was read against the accused: as fear of being caught, and in one case as knowledge of the persona's age. In that case the accused had said so himself.
- That says less than it seems to. Acquittals leave no written opinions. In one case on the question of what the accused believed, the first jury could not agree.
- The argument does have a place in the law. It is the point section 19.3 called resolve. A military appeals court held in 2025 that arriving at the meeting place was not enough to show a firm intent. That case turned on what the accused had with him, not on what he felt.
- For Specs 1 and 2 the nervousness is neutral. The question is still who he believed she was.

### 20.1 What is right in the point

**The room was her proposal.** His first suggestion, at 15:51:36, was to send a car to bring her to him. She declined, and wrote "we have a room" at 15:52:55, less than four minutes after his first message. Section 9.5 sets out the exchange.

**The wariness was not sudden.** The log and the timeline show it building for almost an hour.

| Time on May 1 | What the log or the timeline shows | Note |
|---|---|---|
| 15:51:36 | He offers to send a car for her | Sent before any room or hotel was mentioned |
| 15:52:55 | She answers "we have a room" | The room is her proposal |
| 15:56:39 | She names the hotel | From the log |
| 15:56:50 | "Lol ok." | Eleven seconds later. He says his suspicion came from an earlier experience at the same hotel (section 19.2) |
| 16:01:35 to 16:02:46 | He asks for pictures, then writes "Im convinced lol" | He says the second was sarcasm (section 10) |
| After 16:09:41 | He asks for a photo with a peace sign "so I know real" | From the log |
| Before he arrived | A search for signs of a sting | Slide 8. Its time and its words are not known |
| 16:48:45 | The photo of the door, with the room number, reaches him | He is on scene at 16:48 |
| 16:49 | He tries the car park of the hotel next door | From the timeline |
| 16:51:08 | "Cash right?" | He says this was meant for someone else (section 16.1) |
| 16:53:54 | "Im a little nervous" | Five minutes after the room number |
| 16:56:05 | "Can you walk me upstairs?" | From the log |
| 16:58:19 | He asks her to come to the car, and offers extra | From the log |
| 16:58:57 | She agrees and asks what car he is in. He answers "Black suv" | From the log |
| 17:02 | The texting apps are removed | The arrest time is not in anything I have seen |

**Three things follow**

1. **"Suddenly" undersells his point.** The first sign comes eleven seconds after she named the hotel. That fits what he has said about an earlier experience there. A fear that appeared only at the door would be easier for the government to explain.
2. **He tried three times to have the meeting somewhere other than the room:** the car he offered to send at the start, the request to be walked up, and his own car at the end. The first was sent before any room or hotel was mentioned, so it was not a reaction to this hotel.
3. **He had the room number from 16:48:45.** As far as the log and the timeline show, he did not go to the door in the thirteen minutes before the apps were removed. I do not know where he was when he was arrested.

**What it costs.** The same table shows a man who was wary for an hour and kept going. He drove there, stayed, and went on writing to her. His explanation is compulsion (section 19.2). The government's is that he had decided to take the risk.

### 20.2 What nervousness can and cannot show

**The argument at its best.** The government describes a man carrying out a settled agreement to pay for sex with two girls in a hotel room. The man in the log never wanted the room. He did not go to it when he had the number, he said he was nervous, and he asked twice for her to come to him. A settled arrangement does not look like that.

**Why a fact-finder is unlikely to take it as far as he does**

1. **People are nervous about what they are about to do.** Nobody is nervous about a meeting he has decided not to attend, or about a person he thinks does not exist. The message assumes that someone was there and that he might go up. Section 16.1 made a related point: people do not tell a suspected scammer that they are nervous.
2. **Nervousness has several possible causes, and the message names none.**

   | Cause | Does it fit an intent to go ahead? | Does it bear on her age? |
   |---|---|---|
   | He believed she was 15 (the government's reading) | Yes | Yes |
   | Buying sex is illegal, and the police might be there (his answer in section 6) | Yes | No |
   | A robbery or a set-up (his answer in section 16.1) | Yes, if she turned out to be real | No |
   | Second thoughts about doing it at all | No | No |
   | Nobody real was there | No | No |

   Only he knows which it was, and more than one can be true (section 19.2). The last two are the only ones that do not fit an intent to go ahead. They are also the two that his next messages make hardest to maintain.
3. **What he did next.** Two minutes later he asked her to walk him upstairs. Two minutes after that he asked her to come to the car, and offered extra. When she agreed, he described his car. Each step moved toward a meeting, on terms he felt safer with. The room was her idea. The car was his.
4. **An agreement is not what the government has to prove in the court-martial.** It has to prove an intent and an act (20.3). So showing that nothing was firmly agreed at 15:53:50 does not answer the charge. Section 19.1 already records that the finding of an agreement at that moment says more than the log does. The log is better evidence for that than the nervousness is.
5. **The arrangement did change, and that is worth saying.** What she proposed at 15:53 was two girls in a room. What he proposed at 16:58 was a meeting in his car. Whatever he intended at the end, it was not the arrangement the government says he accepted at the start.
6. **One detail leans his way.** A man afraid of being seen with a 15-year-old is unlikely to ask her to come down and walk him through a hotel. That request fits fear of what was in the room better than it fits fear of being seen with her. It is a small point, and it is for counsel.

**Which charge it reaches**

| Charge | What the government must show | What the nervousness does |
|---|---|---|
| Spec 3 | An intent to obtain a sexual act for money, and an act that strongly corroborates it | It goes to how firm the intent was. It does not remove an intent that depended on her being real (section 19.3). The message about the car is a new offer |
| Spec 2 | An intent to commit a sexual act with her, an act that strongly corroborates it, and a belief that she was under 16 | The same on firmness. Nothing on what he believed, unless the reason for the nervousness is shown |
| Spec 1 | That he sent indecent words to someone he believed was under 16 | Nothing on the words. The message about the car is one of those relied on (section 19.1) |
| Texas | A knowing offer or agreement to pay for sexual conduct | Nothing, if an offer or agreement was made (20.3) |

### 20.3 Where the argument fits in the law

These are my understanding of the rules, for counsel to confirm.

**The court-martial: how firm the intent was**

1. **The elements of an attempt.** An overt act; a specific intent to commit the offense; an act that is more than preparation; and an act that apparently tends to bring the offense about (*United States v. Payne*, 73 M.J. 19 (C.A.A.F. 2013), as quoted in *Penaloza*, below). No agreement is required.
2. **The act has to show a firm intent.** It must be "strongly corroborative of the firmness of the defendant's criminal intent" (*United States v. Byrd*, 24 M.J. 286 (C.M.A. 1987)). It must "unequivocally" show that the crime will take place unless independent circumstances interrupt it (*United States v. Winckelmann*, 70 M.J. 403 (C.A.A.F. 2011)). This is the legal form of his point. Evidence that he would not go to the room is evidence about firmness.
3. **Travel usually satisfies it.** *Winckelmann* said that courts agree that travel is a substantial step in enticement cases. *United States v. Hale*, 78 M.J. 268 (C.A.A.F. 2019), said that a substantial step can be something as ordinary as travel, arranging a meeting or booking a hotel. Service courts have treated the journey to meet the persona as enough for attempted sexual assault of a child (20.4).
4. **But not always.** In *Byrd* the accused took money to buy marijuana for an undercover agent, rode in a taxi to the place where it was sold, and bought liquor there. The court held that the ride was not strongly corroborative of a firm intent. In *Penaloza* the Army court held in 2025 that arriving at the meeting place was not a substantial step where the accused had no cash, and cash was the only payment the persona would accept (20.4).
5. **What that means here.** *Penaloza* turned on what the accused had with him. On the same reasoning the arrest inventory matters more than the message about being nervous. Cash in the amount discussed would point one way, as "Cash right?" and "Ill give extra" already do. No cash, or too little, would point the other. Section 7 already asks for the inventory, and section 16.1 asks whether he drew cash on the way. Section 21.3 applies this to Spec 3.
6. **The specification may not name the act relied on.** An earlier version of this point said that it would, and section 21.1 corrects it. If the act is the drive, the question is what he intended as he drove. What he did on arrival is evidence of that, in both directions. He can say that for thirteen minutes nothing outside him stopped him from going to the room, and he did not go. The government can say that by 16:59 she was on her way down to his car. The government may also rely on what he did at the hotel as an act in itself.

**The court-martial: voluntary abandonment**

1. **Military law allows the defense even after a substantial step.** "It is a defense to an attempt offense that the person voluntarily and completely abandoned the intended crime, solely because of the person's own sense that it was wrong, prior to the completion of the crime" (Manual for Courts-Martial, pt. IV, ¶ 4.c.(4); *United States v. Rios*, 33 M.J. 436 (C.M.A. 1991)). At least one federal appeals court does not allow it once the attempt is complete (*United States v. Young*, 20.4).
2. **The reason has to be the right one.** The same paragraph says the defense "is not allowed if the abandonment results, in whole or in part, from other reasons, for example, the person feared detection or apprehension, decided to await a better opportunity for success, was unable to complete the crime, or encountered unanticipated difficulties or unexpected resistance."
3. **Fear of a scam is not that reason.** It is a difficulty, and not a sense that the thing was wrong. So the nervousness he describes cannot support this defense.
4. **It has to be complete.** In *Rios* the accused gave up one robbery and went on to try another, and the defense was not available. Here the only act that points away from the meeting is the removal of the apps at 17:02, and he stayed where he was (section 16.1). The arrest time decides how much that shows.
5. **It pulls against his account.** A man who says he gave something up is saying that there was something to give up. Counsel can argue in the alternative. Whether he had decided anything at 17:02 is a fact only he knows. This is a statement of what the rule requires, and not a suggestion about what to say. Section 3 called this defense weak, and that is still my assessment.

**Who proposed the room.** It matters for what "Ok" answered (section 9.5). It is not entrapment. Offering a place is an opportunity, and he had opened the conversation.

**Texas: the offer is the offense**

1. **The statute.** A person commits solicitation of prostitution if he "knowingly offers or agrees to pay a fee to another person for the purpose of engaging in sexual conduct with that person or another" (Penal Code § 43.021(a)). Nothing more has to happen. If an offer or agreement was made, what he felt afterwards does not undo it.
2. **The message about the car came after the nervousness.** On its face it is an offer.
3. **A test may not be an answer there.** Under the earlier prostitution statute, the Court of Criminal Appeals held that a person can knowingly offer or agree without intending to go through with it, and that intent to consummate is not an element (*Mattias v. State*, 731 S.W.2d 936 (Tex. Crim. App. 1987)). That case concerned the seller. The buyer's statute has the words "for the purpose of engaging in sexual conduct," and whether an offer made as a test is such an offer is a question for his Texas counsel. Until it is answered, his statement that the extra was offered to expose a scam (section 16.1) should be treated as one that could admit the Texas offense.
4. **Texas has a renunciation defense for attempts,** with a similar limit on motive (Penal Code § 15.04). The charge as described is not an attempt.

### 20.4 The reported cases

**What I looked for.** A decision in which the accused drove to the meeting place and then said he was nervous, on arriving or on being given a room number, and in which the court said what that showed.

**What I found.** None that matches. Section 23.3 has since added a court-martial in which the accused stayed in his car near the meeting place, and section 23.5 says what it shows. The cases below are the nearest. In the sting cases among them the accused was arrested as he arrived, or after he had met the agent. None has what the log shows here: a period of messages sent from the car park, without an approach to the room.

**Cases in which hesitation or nervousness was discussed**

| Case | Facts | How the court read it | How this case differs |
|---|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Puff*, No. 23-4457 (4th Cir. 17 Mar. 2025) | He answered an ad on a prostitution site, using a number from a second-number app. He was told by text that the girl was 13. He asked more than once whether she was the police. He watched the hotel before the meeting. He paid an agent $60. He deleted the app and at first lied about it. He told agents that he knew she was 13, that he was nervous because of her age, and that he had not been nervous in that way with adults | Convictions affirmed. The questions about the police, the watching, the deletion and his own account of his nervousness were evidence that he knew her age | He said it himself. He was told the age in the same conversation and went on. He paid |
| *United States v. Fulsom*, No. 202500166 (N-M. Ct. Crim. App. 29 May 2026) | The persona was listed as 18 and said by text that she was "almos 15." He refused at first and came back within minutes. He spoke of the risk to his career. He asked for a photo "at the moment" and for video calls. He traveled 45 minutes with condoms | Convictions affirmed. His hesitation "stemmed from fear of apprehension, not the lack of predisposition." The requests for proof were counted as showing that he went on knowingly. The journey was a "clear" substantial step | His refusal ended the conversation. Nothing was said about age the next day and, as far as the excerpts show, he was offered no way out then (section 11.3) |
| *United States v. Petersen*, No. 25-13589 (11th Cir. 13 Nov. 2026) | He answered an ad for a 19-year-old and was told she was 14. He asked to meet the next day instead. The agent wrote "u playing." He drove to a gas station and was arrested | Conviction affirmed. His messages showed that he "only wished to avoid detection by police" | The issue was an entrapment instruction. He had been told the age and went on |

**Cases on arriving at the place**

| Case | Facts | Result |
|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Penaloza*, ARMY 20230473 (Army Ct. Crim. App. 28 Feb. 2025) | He arranged to meet a persona he believed was under 18, and to pay cash. He was arrested on arriving. No cash was found on him or in the car | The court held the evidence insufficient. Travel "must be accompanied by evidence of a firm intent to complete the offense," and there was "a fair and rational hypothesis" that he did not intend to pay. I did not see the final order, or check for any later appeal |
| *United States v. Byrd*, 24 M.J. 286 (C.M.A. 1987) | Set out in 20.3 | Riding to the place of sale was not strongly corroborative of a firm intent |
| *State v. Duke*, 709 So. 2d 580 (Fla. Dist. Ct. App. 1998), and *State v. Kemp*, 753 N.E.2d 47 (Ind. Ct. App. 2001) | Each accused arranged sex online with a supposed child and drove to the car park agreed. One flashed his lights as arranged. The other brought condoms | Not enough for an attempt at the sexual offense itself. These are older state decisions and the minority view. They bind no court that will hear this case, and I did not check whether they are still followed |
| *United States v. Ozbirn*, No. ACM 39556 (A.F. Ct. Crim. App.), affirmed on another point, 81 M.J. 38 (C.A.A.F. 2021) | He arranged sex with a supposed 12-year-old and drove to meet her | The journey was a substantial step toward sexual assault of a child |
| *United States v. Rutgerson*, 822 F.3d 1223 (11th Cir. 2016) | He answered an ad and was told she was 15. He tried first to have her come to him. He then drove to her hotel and was arrested | Conviction affirmed. The court noted that he did not hesitate after learning her age |
| *United States v. Young*, 613 F.3d 735 (8th Cir. 2010) | He drove to a motel to meet a supposed minor | Travel was a substantial step. "A defendant cannot abandon an attempt once it has been completed" |
| *State v. Dickerson*, No. 20230751-CA (Utah Ct. App. 2025) | He drove to the place agreed. He argued that many steps remained before any contact | The argument was rejected and the convictions affirmed |

**Two cases close to his in other ways**

| Case | Facts | Result |
|---|---|---|
| *United States v. Medina*, No. 24-1609 (1st Cir. 9 Sept. 2025) | He answered an ad for commercial sex. The agent's texts described girls of 14 and 12. He came to the hotel with $200 and condoms and was given a room key. At booking he was surprised by the charge and denied speaking to any minor. His defense was that his English was limited and that he believed he was arranging to meet an adult | The first jury could not agree. The second convicted. Affirmed |
| *United States v. Munn*, ARMY 20250252 (Army Ct. Crim. App. 2026) | A website sting. He agreed a fee with an officer posing as a sex worker and was arrested on arriving at the hotel. He was charged with attempted patronizing and attempted sexual assault of a child | By agreement with the special trial counsel the case went to a special court-martial. He pleaded guilty to attempted patronizing, and the child specification was dismissed |

**What the cases show**

1. **Courts ask what the accused was afraid of.** In *Fulsom* and *Petersen* the answer was the police. In *Puff* it was her age, in his own words. In each the accused had just been told the age, so a fear of being caught was evidence that he knew what he could be caught for.
2. **His record differs on both points.** Nothing was said about age on May 1. When it was said on April 30 he did not hesitate. He refused. And most of his checks were on whether she was real and whether the room was safe: the pictures, the sign, the request to be walked up. On the deck's own account a check for the police is among them too (slide 8).
3. **The government has a pattern to follow, and *Puff* is that pattern.** A second-number app, a check for police, a look at the hotel, nervousness, an app removed. Each has a counterpart in this case (sections 2 and 9.3).
4. **The answer to *Puff* is in his records.** That accused said he had not been nervous with adults.
